Probably the stall which will attract anglers most is that which lisplays the captures—a few of them—of that fam- ous angler Mr. J. Jardine. The exhibits are enough to turn the ordinary angler wild with envy, and it may be remarked that the fish are not the unedu- cated fish that one would find, say, in Siberia, but fish the most cultured as tG anglers' wiles, from the neighbourhood of London. There is 371 b. pike which has made itself a name in fishing literature. Five perch from the Thames and Colse weighing 121 b. 6 :z. make an exhibit which probably no other liv- ing angler could show. Then we have a grayling of 31b. 12oz. and a dace of lib., taken on the same day from Itches. But the case which would most cause amazement and some other emotions in the Mancl:ester angler who frequents the Derbyshire streams is one of six trout weighing 221 b. 40z., cap- tured on the upper waters of the Colne with the May-fly. By the way a grayling was recently re- ceived at the British Museum weighing over but this was not captured, as anglers would say legitimately.

The fate of Lord Rosebery has descended upon Air. Marjorihanks, the chief Liberal Whip, with dramatic and regretable suddenness. It is a matter of common knowledge that the new Premier recirets the accident of birth which binds him to the House of Lords. During the past few days the position of the able and successful chief Liberal Whip was widely discussed, and conjectures as to whether he would retain his past onerous position or take a place in the new Cabinet were freely hazarded. Now the problem has been solved by an occurence beyond the schemes of man. The first Baron Twerdmouth died rather suddenly on F..aturda evening. Therefore, Mr. Majoribanks that was is now Lord Tweedmouth, with a seat in the House of Lords. A new Whip must be sought for.

The first Lord Tweedmouth was born in 1320. The Majortbanks family hails from Greenlands, Bucks, and the nobleman whose death was announced on Monday morning sat as M.P. for Berwick from 1333 until the peerage was kestowed on him in 1881— with the exception of the six years between 1868 and 1874. It need not be said that such a change of position on the part of so important a member of the Liberal party as Mr. Edward Majoribanks, especially at this juncture, is one of the great events of the day, and may be productive of consequences affecting the entire nation..

it may now be said with practical certainty that there will be nothing in the shape of a Unionist amendment to the Address, challenging the exist- ence of the Government. The Liberal Unionists will have a meeting early next week, and will pro- bably settle then whether anything is to be done in regard to the matter of Irish local government and the general question of Irish administration. Bat there is so little expectation of a critical party fight at the beginning of the session that I believe some members are counting n being able to pair from the middle of next week until after Easter. ln point of fact the Opposition will be only too glad to allow matters to proceed smoothly for the first week or two, as the strain of political excitement has told heavily on the leading men on both sides; and, apart from the natural desire for sOme relief, there is a very strong feeling that the political situation renders it necessary to pay some attention to the campaign in the constituencies.

The most important speeches of the next week or two are more likely to be delivered out of Parha- • ment than in. There is a sort of general under- standing that if the Opposition facilitate the obtaining of the necessary supplies,no controversial matter will be taken in hand until after the intro- duction of the Budget. The Registration Bill may be read a first time, but that is probably all. The • Budget itself is looked forward to with the utmost hope on the Liberal benches, and there will cer- tainly he keen disappointment if it does not prove to be of a thoroughgoing democratic character. The Opposition at all events fully anticipate that it will be so, and they are glad to reserve their forces for what they believe will be the first grcat—and pro- bably the most critical—struggle of the session. On both sides it is believed that from Easter onwards , this will be one of the hardest and most severely fought sessions of modern times. Both are pre- par'ed for the possibility that the conflict may cul- minate in a dissolatiui somewhere about Whit-sun- tide, but it is universally agreed that if this does I not come about, we are in for another session of ab- normal length. Registration and. Welsh .Disesta- blishment—to take only two of the measures which the Government have in hand—are not to be dealt with in a day, and to get the latter measure through I will probably need an autumn sitting.

" CAPITAL" CHAT,

Mr. Chamberlain has declined to follow Mr. Labouchere's example. The member for Northamp- ton engaged in a verbal duel with Mr. Hyndman before his constituents at Northampton. The con- test was interesting, but, as was to be expected, hardly decisive. However, it served a good pur- pose. It gave the people of Northampton some- thing to think about,and it enabled the combatants to understand each other better if it did not bring them into agreement. Mr. Chamberlain has shirked a similar duel over "contracting out" with Mr. Sam Woods, M.P.,on the ground that the discussion would be " a waste of energx.' Most people believe that the member for Birmingham has expended a considerable amount of energy much less profitably since 1886.

A London correspondent t,113 another story of Mr. Gladstone," The last time I sa w him," he writes "was at a party given by Lady Harcourt in Down- ing-street just before Christmas. He was standing some paces within the doorway, and alter his cus- tomery gracious salutation, he said to me, looking at a figure in the doorway, "Is that young Cham- berlain standing over there ?" The person indicated was not a bit like the member for East Worcester- shire, a circumstance which testified to the grow- ing indistinctness of eyesight. The incident was characteristic of Mr. Gladstone's generous nature. He was afraid to appear to slight the son of an old friend by passing him without recognition, and had evidently been troubling himself for a moment or two in the attempt to identify the gentleman upon whom his eyes had rested." Yet it is told that Mr. Gladstone once embittered an influential supporter by appearing not to recognise him in the Lribby of the House.

It appears to be more than doubtful whether any necessity will arise for Mr.Labouchere's amendment to the Address on the subject of the House of Lords. I learn on excellent authority that the Queen's Speech will contain a paragraph dealing with the action of the Second Chamber that cannot leave any doubt as to the attitude of the Government. So far as the Lords are concerned, I am credibly informed that the position of the new Cabinet is cne of un- compromising opposition. Mr. Courtenay Warner and Mr. Charles Fenwick are the selected two mem- bers of the House of Commons to propose and second the Address in answer to the Queen's Speech in the House of Commons. Mr. Fenwick has been specially chosen as he represents the trades unions, and is noted for industrial interest in the working man.

A mintle of midis* silk, beaded wee- mentirie, and double capes of mrror velvet Is very handsome. The capes have the flat tiffectt en the shoulders, to accompany large sleeves in the present drooping fashion. From dress to hats is no very great step, and they are generally closely allied. The fashion for susp6nding our head-gear above our heads has not yet entirely passed into the oblivion whisk it deserves. A delightful eombination of colour which I met in a bonnet to-day was a deep-sapphire blue, with voilets. Thia may sound atrocious but It was simply beautiful. it was on a bonnet ;hick was entirely made of the velvet, with launched of the violets set at each corner, and there were three corners, pointing tewards the middle, the ethers on either side. A large bow of velvet was net across the back, etberwise it was guileless of trint- ming,.

A new hat is, of course, a necessity when the sun once more puts in an appearance, and a becoming shape for most faces is of black chip, for chip is to be worn again, and a curved steel buckle fastens the satin ribbon which encircles the crown with two upstanding black tips on one side. Beneath the brim some pink blossoms rest with picturesque effect on the hair.

A very effective bodice has the baaque, deep cuffs, and revers of silk covered with black guipure. Very dainty contrasts may be obtained in this fashion, or a dull or unbecoming gown may be brightened most advantageously. Both craam, ecru, or black lace can be used in this style, and salmon-pink or solferino covered with black guipure is bright and pretty on a blctok gown. Dan stone or grey shades may have turquoise silk and ecru lace. Heliotrope looks well with petunia silk ana a very deeply tinted lace, or fawn and 12/9.5.x1i phades with rose, heliotrope, or blue silk and a fay deep ooffeg or ecru lace.

A pretty dress is made, in a pale shade of blue. The embroidered bands which outline the round yoke and cross at the back also border the arm- holes, and are made of the satin embroidered in seed pearls, while soft white gauze forms the sleeves and full-flowing front.

Bordon laces are at present the most fashionable ; they have a wired cord, and the patterns are geometrical rather than floral. They are used for flounces in three widths, and for mantles. Chan- tilly with wired grounds and much jet embroidery upon it is also used, and check nets' such as hitherto we have only associated with veilings, have been worked with jet over moire or silk, and are to be used greatly for yokes to dresses and mantles. Flouncings are now sold gathered to their headings in wide and narrow widths, so that there is no qu,:stion as to the amount to use in the fulness.

A dainty frock of muslin and :Insertion, suitable for girls from two to 10 years of age, has the muslin set full into a square yoke, which is hidden by the deep, graceful collar. This is ornamented with rows of insertion, and edged with double flounces of some pretty, cheap lace. The bottom of the skirt is vandyked with insertion, which forms the heading to deep flounces of lace matching the collar. This frock must be made up over a plain foundation; while the style of garment may be copied in many and various materials, such as cashmere, nun's veiling, silk, or crepon. Yours as ever,

JENNIN.

Miss Alice Goodhall, editor of the Guardian," pub- lished in Simla,, is said to be the only woman elitor in India. She. is an Eni,,,lishwomars, fair, and not yet forty. Miss Frances Crosby, author of "Safe in the Arms of Jesus" and other widely sung Gospel hymns, is sixty- one years of age, and has been Wind since she was six weeks old.

The real name of Ada Cambridge, whose novel, "A Marriage Csremony," is creating some stir, is Mrs. Cross. She is the wife of the vicar of one of the fashionable Me- lbourne churches, and is at present in Australia. News comes from Nice that Mary Anderson Navarro, who is staying there,lives very quietiy,neither receiving nor paying visits. The cause assigned is very delicate health, a condition,in fact,bordering on the dreaded and obstinate one of nervous prostration. The clergy of the little town of Eschenbach, in Fran coma, have issued a solemn protest against kissing on the stage as being "an immoral practice, which would give a bad example and be regarded by the spectators as sanctioning all manner of disorders." Miss Rachel Frank, a young Californian, will be the first woman rabbi ever ordained in the Hebrew Church im any country. She as studied at the Hebrew College in Cincinnati, and is taking an extra course of study be- ion her ordination. There is much speculation as to what title she will be known by, and what robe she will wear, as there is absolutely no precedent to apply in this cve.

The German Empress was during her stay at Felix- stowe so much impressed by the perfections of the British nursemaid bien 6levo that shortly after her re- turn to Germany, she commissioned an English lady. friend to engage an English nurse for the German Imperial children. One ot the conditions under which the nurse was engaged was that she was not to be told, until the engagement was settled, who her charges would be. This, the Empress considered, was the only way of obtaining a thoroughly competent nurse, and not a personage who could come for the s Ake of the posi- tion merely, and in order to be able to trade for ever after on the gloriesof her office.

In rraise of Oatmeal. Dr. Johnson, who disliked Scotsmen and things Scot- tish, defined " oats," in his " dictionary,' as " Food for men in Scotland and for horses in England." But noth- ing says" Little Folks" has been heard about the defini- tion since the famous retort of a Scottish judge :—"Yes and where will you find sti,h men and such horses ?" Carlyle believed in oatmeal,for he remarked of Macauley " Well, anyone can see that you are an honest good sort of fellow, made out of oatmeal." That it is not only nourishing, but filling, rat the price' was proved by the shrewd Scotswoman who used to tell her five bairns that " the one that ate the waist parritch would get the maist meat." And so, when the meat came, there was no room for it. The most curious fact about oatmeal is, however, that the use of porridge is on the decrease in Scotland,and on the increase in England. This looks bad for the Scotsmen of the future.

Miss Emily Faithfull. Why should not women be educated, as their brothers are, for regular professions ? Why should all this educa- tion be a question of sex ? Let it be one of fitness, rather. Why should the girl starve, and the brother be in a good position ? Girls must be trained for their I after-life. As a rule, they have no practical training, and only begin to work when they need money, and have already reached the time of life when they ought to be reaping the fruits of past toil instead of beginning to build up a future. Some years ago, writing to the " Daily News," I urged three points which must be insisted on with regard to the employment of our girls : —(1) Their need of definite training. (2) The best work at fair market prices, must be given. Lasting benefit can only be reaped by thorough work and sub- mission to the conditions which alone make it possible. For waist of this,l have seti'd the careers of many women wrecked. (3) As I hP:ve just said, senseless social p re- judief:4 bei hbandoned. We have overcome such -erejuaicea in the case of men, and ladies must have the courage to go t) business, and not seek help through " Work Societies," in which they are known by some number, and their real name concealed. But in all cases the worker arlst be trained; untrained work is only paid for as a charity, and the remuneraticA is sure to be low. —" The Quiver."

. . , Many plans of diet Oiablt eesi.t."7l l reCom.m-ended in tha treatment of obesity. They all agree in a considerable limitation of the amount of starchy and saccharine food prescribed—dry toast, biscuits, or rusks being recom- mended in place of ordinary bread, milk, and sugar being prohibited altogether, and vegetables strictly limited in amount. By some muvli importance is at- tached to the strict limitation of the quantity of liquid, particularly at meals ; others recommend that plenty of tea, coffee, -xater, and the like should be con- sumed : but nearly all agree in the recogaition of the advimbility of taking 'Jut little alcohol. Most authori- ties reeonimand that meat should be taken in small quantities, and not more than °nee a day, While there are various views as to the quality of the food taken, there is a tolerably general consensus of opinion as to the quantity ; and it is the height of folly for a patient to imagine that he can (by avoiding a -few particular articles of diet) at one and the same time indulge largely in the pleasures of the table and grow thin. The famous formula of Mr. 13antine:, by which that gentle- man was able to reduce his weight to the extent of nearly three stone in the course of a year, has been adopted by many people since his time. He recom- mended Fr breakfast, some 6 or 7 oz. of solid food (meat, or fish and toast), and 9 oz. of liquid (tea or coffee without milk or sugar) ; for dinner, 10 or 12 oz.of solid food, witn 10 oz. of wine ; for tea, 2 to 4 oz of solid food (fruit and a rusk or tw.) with 9 oz. of tea ; and for supper, 3 to 4 cz. of solid food, with 7 oz. of wine. This diet probably goes to an extreme in the ex- clusion of fat, and is somewhat liberal in the matter of alcohol. It suits some people, hut it is advisable that a seveie regimen should not be adopted save under medi- cal advice.—" Cassell's Storehouse of General Informa- tion.'"

DID SHAKSPEAR,E KNOW ANYTHING?

When we come to think or it,he could not have known much, not even how it feels to wear a set of artificial teeth. He never even saw a Weiler match or a steel pen, and his ignorance of the art of photography was only equalled by the lack of knowledge he possessed ab3ut the type writer. If he went to a fire it was probably only to stand in a line and pass buckets of water,the fire engine being something he was entirely unacquainted with. He never held his ear to a telephone and listened to his wife while she read out a list of little articles sh e wished him to bring home; he never rode in a tramcar or in a railway train, and as for an electric rail way,why he had never heard of such a thing. He never knew the feelings which a boy experiences when wearing long trousers for the first time, because he never wore such things in his life. He never tried to ride a bicycle,never used roller fArates,never saw a stova,knew nothing about such well-known things as steam ploughs and reaping machines. He never knew what it was to swallow a postage stamp by mistake while moistening it (not be- cause be could not swallow, but, because he had never seen a postage stamp), and we have yet to learn that he could tell the difference between an eighty-one ton gun and a repeating rifle. The question is, what did he know ?—" Cassell's Saturday Journal."

THE DOUBLE LIFE,

OR, THE HAMPTON MYSTERY.

By the Author of " DARKWOOD."

CHAPTER HI. LORD ADLOWB BEGINS RIB QUEST. He knew himself detested—but he know That hearts that loathed him crouched ant; dreaded, too..

THE CORSAIR

Too nervous to rid'e, Lord Mlowe proceeded on foot along the street, through the wind and storm, protected from the rain by overcoat and umbrella. His mind was absorbed in the mystery that had been so recently brought under his notiee. He walked along like one in a trance. Suddenly he started and paused. Hie eyes flashed through the gloom with vigorous determination. He had come to a conclusion—decided upon a course of action. "I'll do it he muttered. "Within a week I can make myself master of Beatrice's secret. I'll set some one on her track at once."

He moved onward, hastening -to his hotel, and to his own chamber.

The room, parlor and bed-chamber in one, was dimly lighted. An air of chilly dampness and dis- comfort prevailed. "What a contrast to Hampton House!" Lis lordship exclaimed. "I am tired of all this gen- teel misery! I should like a home of my own, and some one to wait on me. Now that I have dis- missed that stupid valet of mine, I am likely to fare worse than ever."

He pulled at the bell furiously, and 4then threw off his outer garments. He had scarcely seated himself when a waiter entered the room in response to his summons, bringing a letter in his hand. "A letter for your lordship," he said, laying his missive on the table. "A man brought it while you were out, and left word he would call again this evening." "Very well," said Lord Adlowe, writing a few lines on the back of a card he had just withdrawn from his card-case and proceeding to inclose the bit of pasteboard in an envelope. "I want a letter sent directly. See there is no delay about it. Any visitor for me is to be shown up at once."

He sealed the envelope, addressing it to "Mr. James Rush, Private Detective," and placed it in the hands of the waiter, with a couple of half- crowns, ensjoining the utmost haste in the delivery of the missive.

The servant withdrew, and Lord Adlowe settling himself in his chair, said musingly : "This problem will be quickly and easily solved. Rush is the best detective in London—a perfect bloodhound ! lie will ferret out Lady Beatrice's secret." As he smiled in anticipated triumph, his wander- ing glances rested upon the letter the waiter hati brought. " Who's this from ? " he thought, stretching out his hand for it. "Ah ! "

He started abruptly, as his gaze fell upon the adaess. It was in an illiterate handwriting, and ill-spelled, but the very sight of it had power to blanch his cheeks and bring an expression of min- gled surprise and annoyance to his face. "It is from Negwyn he exclaimed in a whis- per. "He brought it himself ! What evil fate has brought him back to England, and at this time? I thought he was dead 1" He tore open the letter with trembling n,ers. A sheet of coarse blue paper, covere‘ f w.ith an illiterate scrawl, fell into his h"-cicla. He read it over, half 'aloud: Its contr- t,9 it as follows: My LORD— A you see by the date of this r kere hist Thurs- day from Australia. Altheuga yoti O'N:T fitt.tw hundred pounds to start me in life eigh&tif tMI Ivo, and 1 started in the farming business in Ans. trali4t difectly after, I am as poor as Lazarus to- day. worked my- passage home to England. This morning I saw your arrival from the East noticed in the paper, with your address, and I write these lines in case you are out when I call. I have. come home to lead an easy life. I want money and comforts. You, my lord, must give them to me. You will not dare refuse them to me, for I have a secret in my possession which Lord Trove'. yan would give a fortune to know. I shall come to you first. My object in coming back to England is to trade on this subject. " Your friend, if you wish it, 6 eorge Negwyn." The letter, whose sense we have given literally, but upon whose spelling we have improved, dropped from Adlowe's nerveless hands.

" The fellow is in a threatening mood t" he mut- tered. "I must conciliate him ! It will never do to let him go to my uncle ! " A heavy shadow settled upon his brow, and he sank into a gloomy reverie, from which he was finally roused by a knoc4, at the door. Crumpling up the letter, and thrusting it into his pocket, he uttered a summons to enter.

The door opened, admitting is visitor—the private detective to whom hi :s lordship had sent a written message an hour before. "Come in, Rush," said the nobleman, not arising from his seat, as he recognized the man whom he had not seen for years. " You got my note, I see, and are as punctual a 3 ever. Be seated." Rush accepted the invitation.

lie was a mall, wiry man, with quick and cat- like movements and an appearance of intense ner- vousness. his thin face had a peculiarly vacant look, whenever he chose te have it so, and his small, been eyes had a habit of moving about rest- lessly, Seeing in those quick glances, one would have thought. Those who knew Mr. Rush best, however, said that nothing ever escaped his vision, and that any circumstance,. however trivial, that came under his 01)servation was sot forgotten, as is usual with online iv people, but was labelled, ticketed, and put awo,y in his capacious, brain, ready for use should occasion ever require it.. In fact, Mr. hash was • a detective of singular ability and modesty--a man who had little connec- tion or acquaintance with police otlici:tls, and who was mainly employed by private individuals who desired to keep their affairs secret.. " Still a detective, I suppose, Rush?" said his

lordship, with a business air. sent the letter to your old address, not 'knowing whether you stil) lived there, or where_ lam glad to see you." The detective bowed gravely. " Some years since," continued Lord Adlowe, "I employed you to look up Geoffrey Trevclyan, after his Bight to London from Trevelyan Park. You traced him to Liverpool, and discovered that he sailed for South America' and subsequently • earned that the same vessel did not bring him back. He died in Brazil, believe before the vessel left Rio. You siiowed great skill in your ' search for him, and maintained a secrecy like that •of the grave. I have need of the same qualities now !"

" I am at your lordship's service," replied the detective.

"You have heard of the Lady Beatrice Hampton,. of course " began. Lord Adlowe, in some embarass- men t.

The detective brightened and settled his wander- ing gaze upon hislost. "Every one has heard of the Earl of Hampton's daughter, my lord," he answered, " the beautiful, unapproachable Lady Beatrice."

" Yes, and wondered at her persistency in re- maining single," said Lord Adlowe. " She has had suitors by the score, but she has refused them all. Now I want you to discover why she does not 1115 rry. " The detective arched his brows, but remained silent.

" You think the task difficult—impossible," said his lordship, " but I can give you a clew that may assist you. I have dined this evening at Hampton House, and have made a discovery that cannot fail ! to awaken your professional zeal." He graphically narrated his adventures of the even- ing, the conversation he had overheard, the visit to the chambers of the Lady Beatrice, and the start- ling discovery of he-r absence. "I have long been a suitor of the lady," he added, 1‘ and renewed my proposal this evening for her hand. She has at last deigned to encourage my suit. I have, therefore, a right to understand her thoroughly, to gain her secrets in any way I can. Will you undertake the case ? " The detective had listened without an interrup- tion, but that he had beert deeply interested in the narrative was evidenced by his reply. "I will work up the case," he said, and his eyes gleamed with professional shrewdness. "You may rely upon me, my lord. I will be as silent and as secret as death. The points are—" "To discover where she goes when she absents herself from home, who she sees, and What she does. Probe her secret to the bottom, and you may name your own reward. Here is a retaining fee." He tossed, the detective at bank-note, which he

took from his purse. Rush received it without looking at it, and put it in his pocket-book. "I am going to Trevelyan Park to-morrow, to see my uncle," said his lordship, "but shall return to town in a day or two. My address, until you hear further, will be at this hotel. I think I shall keep these rooms for the season, dividing my tiine between london and Trevelyan Park."

"I wiU send you a report of my proceedings every day, my lord," said Rush. "If I have any- thing of great importance to communicate, I win fetch the news myself. Is there anything more?" "Nothing more, I believe." "Bas the Lady Beatrice a maid?" "Really, I don't know. I think not. In fact, I sm sure not. "She is still absent from home?"

14rd Adlowe replied in the affirmative." "I will commence my duties at once," remarked Rush, arising. "Fortunately, I have nothing pressing on my hands now. I will pay every attention to this business, and keep you informed of my discoveries. My lord, good evening."

The detective bowed himself out, closing the door behind him.

"The case is in the right hands," muttered Ad- lowe, delightedly, when again alone. "By next week, at the latest, I shall know where the Lady Beatrice spends her missing hours. I hope Rush will make some horrible discovery of an intrigue,, or some such thing ! I'd use it to tame that haughty creature- I'd give a fortune to see lbsi, bow and plead to me—to me whom she detests,. but whose case the has deigned to take into con- sideration. lam on the threshold of a splendid revenge for her coldness and slight--on the thres- hold of a glorious triumph." He enjoyed his prospect with the ardour of a, petty soul. "And it's time," he added, after a pause, that my long and sa-earisorne courtship ended in marriage; I am in debt, and need money. My unck, Lord Trevelyan, seems bound to live forever. Ali'!"

He started, as a low, irregular knocking sounded on the door. The next moment a F.4cond visitor entered the room.

He was a man a little past the middle age, tall; and stout; and strong, with grizzled hair and! beard, an unquiet eye, bespeaking a mind ill at] ease, and his general aspect was that of one whol had been buffeted by the world. His garmentsi were seedy and threadbare. His manner was more] defiant than respectful. His name, as the letter he had sent indicated, was George Negwyn. He had been, eighteen years before, the valet and confidential attendant of Lord Adlowe, and had emigrated to Australia„ where he had since remained.

Lord Adlowe greeted his former valet with an easy nod, and a smile of forced pleasantness.

"So you've turned up again, Negwyn, he ob- served, pointing to a chair. "I thought you promised to remain in Australia for the term of your natural life, and her you are again Two hundred pounds was to he a quit-claim upon inel and now you want more ! " "I can't help it, my lord," said the visitor, sul- lenly, as he sat down, twirling his old slouched hat in his hands. "I ain't going to starve, my lord, when I know somethin -that others 'ivould pay to know also. If yo want to treat with me, just say so. I ;IA ready to go at once!" and ha half arose frdn his chair. - • "You heean't be iii_a_l2i_irrz,'Yo-bserved Lord Adidev quiey.- 'TT suppose I refuse to do any- o- Vilna tir 17'a—what then?" _

"I shall go down to Trevelyan Park by the first train," was the response. "Or, perhaps I'd call on the Lady Beatrice Hampton first. She would give something to have Geoffrey Trevelyan's nanê An 'f she wouldn't, Lord Trevel- M -v:tonld pay well a the truth in a cer- tain matter that transpired just eiihteen years ago." " Well, what is your demand, ilegWYlll What. do you want ? " asked his lordship, surveying his visitor with asshrned carelessness.

"I want a home—money—clothes !" exclaimed the man, with a burst of bitterness. • "There are nights when I cannot sleep for thinking of one we both wronged—you in deed ;Iby my silence. I heard that he was dead—that he died in Brazil. My lord, do you never think remorsefully of that poor boy—ruined, _wrecked, and dead? Ile might have• been living now, happy and honoured, but for you and me ! "

The visitor's hard, st;rong features twitched con- vulsively, and he 100he.4 with ea,gerness.at his lord- ship. 'No ; I never think such absurdities," declared Adlowe, disdainfully. "And if you do, you are a weak and pining fool; not a man of sense." Negwyn clasped his brown hands together, and dismissed all emotion from his face.

" Pardon, my lord," he said, half humhly. " I have that mood come over me at times, but I can be true to a compact, if you choose tc•make one with me."

"About money," said Adlowe, deliberately—" I have little to give. My own small property is al- ready enedmbered with debts. My only hope is in Lord Trevelyan's death ! But I'll make, you. an Negwyn." " Well ?" said the visitor, inquiringly. "I will take you back into my service as my. ralet. I have none at present. You shall have a salary of a hundred pounds a year, and at Lord Trevelyan's death I will give you a thousand pounds down. What do you say to that ?" "I accept the offer, my lord," was the eager re- ponse,. " When shall I enter upon my new duties? "

" Immediatel,7," replied his lordship, producing a twenty-pound note from his pocket-book and tossing it to his visitor. "Go out and fit yourself up with suitable clothes with that. To-morrow / go down to Trevelyan Park for a brief visit to my uncle. You will accompany me. No one will recognise you there, for you have changed greatly. Yet you had better take a new name. I don't want all my past raked over, as it would be by the vil- lage gossips, or by my uncle, if you are known to be in my service again. Besides," he added, " the Lady Beatrice Hampton has advertised for you again and again. Ido not wish her to know that you still live !"

" Then, my lord, you can call me Hasidim," said the newly engaged valet. "I was known by that name part of the time out in Australia. I don't care to see the Lady Beatrice. She wouldn't pay me so well as your lordship will." 44 No, because she has not the same reason to do • • Be faithful to me, Negwyn, and you won't re- gret it. The valet picked up the bank-note and put it in his pocket. An air of contentment and satisfac- tion now beamed from his hard-featured face, re- placing his former expression of defiance. lie arose, saying: "I'll go out now, my lord, for new clothing. I will be back in an ;lour. Your lordship 'll hear no more whining from me. Even if Geoffrey Trevel- yan was to come back—and it is possible, isn't it, that he is not dead—l would stand by your lordship. You have made me your slave, my lord ! " _

He retired from the room with a succession of

bows. Lord Adlowe looked after him with thoughtful glance. "Yes, I have made Negwyn my slave," he mused. "And if Geoffrey Trevelyan should come back— God help him I But I need not waste thoughts on one who has mouldered to dust. I will think, in- stead, of the peerless beauty whom I shall soon claim as my own. The cords will soon begin to tighten around he-. Ah !my Lady Beatrice, you may be keen, but you have got a desperate man to deal with now. And as surely as you and I both live, that bloodhound of a detective will know your secret before you are many days—yes, hours—. OM I

(To be continued).

Lord Justice A. L. Smith occasionally sits with his brother magistrates at Chichester. Recently some new JP's were sworn and took their seats on the bench for the first time: One of them was noticed to be coach- ing the Lord Justice throughout the whole of the proceedings.

SPRING.

O'er heaven's blue arch, so wide, so deep, Spring's fleecy clouds appear;. And fresh from winter's gloomy sleep, Earth hails the vernal year. The days expanding brighter grow, • As en they gently glide: How beautiful the morning's glow, How calm the eventide! River and lake, and sedgy pond All sparkle in the sun: While streamlets freed from icy bond, Glad through the valle3 a run. 07er hill and dale, and desert land, Soft blows the balmy air; And wand'ring by the lonely strand, Plays with the wavelets there. The woods their leafy charms display So rich in varied hues; The blossom'd hedgerow by the way, Our path with fragrance strews. Now, first of all the early flowers, The daisy breaks the mould: : And, laughing, drinks the genial showers. That fill her cups of gold. Wbere'er we tread, where'er we rest, That lowly fower is been: To age and childhood still the best, To mem'ry aver green. What new-b3rn insects on the wing. Disport them in the grove On thorn and tree the wild birds sing Their ow sweet songs of love. And man, afresh to labour born, Goes forth with morning now,, To sow the fields with yellow corn, Or guide the rustic plough. Though bard his task, light is his heart And as he strides along, He cheers his humble, useful part, With snatch of rural song. Hail glorious Spring, we love thy reign, Its charms have few alloys: Thy touch renews oar youth again, Reviving hopes and joys. And what if thunder crashing loud Should wake some nervous fears? gow soon upon the passing cloud The bow of peace appears! Its. H. W. CLINDINNING.

THE MASSACRE' OF WILSON'S PARTY,

,0....•

THE 2..AST STAND. THE SCENE OF

CAPE TOWN, MONDAY.-Mr. Dawst:"l, the trader, Whi has just returned fr.,m his mission beyotii *he Shangani for the purpose of ascertaining the truth regPfethig the death of Lobenguli, and recovering the remains Of Major Wilson's party, has arrived at Buluwayo, having ac- complished the objects of his journey. He has now telegraphed his official report to Sir Henry Loch. Mr. Dawson states that he made his way across the Shan- gani river to the scene of the last stand of Wilson, Bor- row, and their comrades. There, within a circle of fif- teen yards, he found thirty-three skulls, and Qne skull was lying some thirty yards outside this narrow circle. Collecting, the skulls and such retnains as were lying near, Mr. Dawson laid them together in one grave under- neath a tree. At the head of the grave he placed a wooden cross, inscribing on it the words," To brave men." As to the death of Lobengula there is no longer room for doubt. Mr. Dawson ascertained that the King died of fever on the 23rd of January, at a spot some forty miles south of the Zambesi. He was nearly alone. The Matabele, Mr. Dawson says, are hurrying into surrender, and are inquiring eagerly where their may live, and what is to be done regarding the support of Lobengula's children and his queens. They have been assured by the British authorities that the wives and families of the Kin, will be well looked after. The tone of the nation, Mr. Dawson adds, is surprisingly meek, and there is no necessity to send forward any troops to complete the subjugation of the Matabele.

In the cause- of charity I am always libcral. What were you going to get?" Daugh- ter : "Something in the way of a new gown." . "IT isn't love," observes "Top," in the Tropical Tim, " that mikes people marry." "It's flattery—rank flattery. The man is pleased because the woman took a fancy to so inferior a creature as be knows himself to be; and the woman's vanity is tickled for a similar reason. In a word, each approves of the other for showing poor taste in choosing a mate." __

The CHAIRMAN said a communication had been received from the Mayor(Alclerman Greenall) saying that he was very much improved in health. (Hear, hear.) , Alderman SUTTON said ho had no doubt that every member of the council wohld'feel highly gratified at the welcome announcement which the Chairman had just made. He proposed a resolution in which the Council expressed their pleasure at the restoration to health of the Mayor, and expressing their hope that the improve- ment would be of a permanent character ; and that he would soon be able to continue his valuable services on behalf of the Corporation and the town generally. (Hear hear.) Alderman Housirs said he had great pleasure in second- ing the resolution. The motion was then carried. TEE AUDITORS. _

The CHAIRMAN reported that Mr. James Bolton, of 63, Wilderspool-road, accountant, and Mr. John Harrison, 4, Egypt-st., tailor and woollen draper,had been elected elective auditors, and that the Mayor had appointed Mr Councillor Arthur Bennett as his auditor.

Alderman HOLMES moved the adoption of the minutes of the Finance and Estate Committee. In having the increase in the work of that department, he said that in the year /877, before the Gas Works were purchased, the number of assessments were 8,2E5, aud the ratable value '£110,063 103. ; in 1893 the assessments were 11,600, and the rat- able value £159,783 10s., or an increase of 4D per cent. of assessments and 46 per cent, in the ratable value. The bonded debt of the Corporation in 1877 was £89,900 and the number of bondholders 263. In 1893 the bonded debt was £574,958. and the number of bondholders and annuitauts over 800. They had recently adfied to the Finance Department the collection of the water rate,and had brought the staff from the offices in Cairo-street to the offices at the Town Hall. In 1889, when they pur- chased the Water ViTerks, there were 11,850 assessments, now there were 13,230. The natural increase had gone on, and the work had increased necessarily, but the staff had not been increased. Even during the last nine months there had been ZOO houses built in the borough, and about 700 additional water assessments during the same period. SCHOOL ATTENDANCE C Olf MI TT EE .-TEU A N T SCHOOL

ACCOMMODATION.

At a meetinc, of the School Attendance Committee it was resolved CO draw the attention of the County Coun- cil of. Lancashire to the necessity of truant school ac- commodation in Lancashire. The average attendance at the public elementary schools during November, December, and January was reported to he :—November 8.143; December, 7,797; January, 8,298; November, 1892, 7,270; December, 1892, 7,120; January. 1893, 7,917; or increases respectively of 873, 677. and 381. It was resolved that the resignation of Councillor Smet- hurst as deputy-chairman of the committee be accepted, and that Councillor Shaw be appointed deputy-chairman. At a further meeting of the' committee, the resolution appointing Councillor Shaw as deputy-chairman was rescinded. Councillor SHAW GREEN moved that the minutes be confirmed with the exception of the resolution appoint- ing Councillor Shaw deputy-chairman cf the committee. He said the Council would notice that there was a reso- lution calling the attention of the County Council of Lancashire to the necessity of truant school accommo- dation. It would be within the recollection of the Coun- cil that they had urged that accommodation of this kind should be provided, and that they had asked the repre- sentatives of the borough on the County Council to take every means in their power to get the matter satisfac- torily arranged. Every boy who was beyond the con- trol of his parents in Warrington had to be sent to the Clio, or other industrial schools, and to be kept there for several years. If they had a truant school where the boy could be sent for a few weeks or months, and then al- lowed to go home again on a certificate, the cost would be small, and the boy might be reclaimed much more readily than if he were sent far away for a long period. Councillor PLUMPTON seconded the confirmation of the minutes. Councillor BOLTON and Alderman Harrison asked for more definite information with regard to the resolution referring to Councillor Shaw's appointment as vice-chair- man of the committee. Alderman HARRISON asked if Councillor Shaw had been appointed deputy chairman of the committee by the Committee? It was stated that Councillor Shaw had been appointed whereupon a discussion arose cas to the legality of rescinding the appointment. The Toerii CLERIC, in reply to several members, said that the committee had the power to rescind any reso- lution they had passed at a previous meeting, and any appointment they made did not require the confirmation of the Council. The School Attendance Committee was like the Watch Committee and the Museum Committee, the proceedings of which were not submitted for the confirmation of the Council except in the matter of money. Councillor Ronears observed that if the chairman and vice-chairman of a committee occupied their positions at the will of the members of the committee,the General Purposes Committee, which consisted largely of the chairmen and deputy chairmen of committees, wculd be subject to considerable changes.

TOWN HALL, PARK, AND BATHS COMMITTEE: THE ,GATES. This Committee's minutes which we published last week, related to the Town Hall entrance gates, pillars, and arch. Councillor HEATON seconded._ _

Councillor FLOOD was disappointed that there was not a fuller explanation with regard to the items of expendi- ture in connection with the gates. They were told a month ago that it was possible the .Council would be called upon to spend £550 for the pulling down of the wall and the formation of the ground in front of the Town Hall, and he now found as an instalment the sum of £369 for the simple work of putting the gates up, which left a small balance to lay out the ground and put up a palisading. That sum would not suffice for the purpose if they carried out the elaborate scheme propQsed. He thought they were entitled to an explanation as to what the expense would run before they granted any more moneyte the committee on that work. The CHAIRMAN said £550 had already been granted for the purpose, and these two items were part of that sum. Councillor Morrus moved an amendment that the minutes be passed with the exception of the portion re- ferring to the rental of £5 per stall at the stables. Ho thought the Sanitary Works Committee should have been consulted before such a resolution wag put on the minutes. Councillor BOLTON said they did not want to make anything out of the stables, but they wished each com- mittee to pay its own rents.—The minutes, except the 'portion referring to the stables, were passed.

The above should speak volumes to the workman. I venture to say there is not a railway, not a coal pit, nor yet many manufacturing concerns that would not yield much more than a "living wage" were they worked from an honest base.

Is there not something rettea in the state when one can read the following in the " Liverpool Post," and yet know that the people are starving: "Now while it is un- doubtedly true that British agriculture is in a deplor- able condition, it is also madeniable that, acre for acre, Great Britain is the most productive country ia the world. She grews more bushels of wheat to the acre, and more pounds to the bushel, than any other c.iuntry on the glebe." Northville, Worcester, Mass. Feb. 9, 1894.

A CHAT WITH A CYCLING CHAMPION.

" Do the long hours required for a big race, and thp monotony of going round the track, affect you ?" asked a " Chums " representative of Mr. F. W. Shorland, the Cycling Long Distance Champion of England. " Not at all," Mr. Shorland replied ; " it may make some riders a little giddy—that is all." "Do you take any special form of nourishment during the twenty-four hours' race against time ?" " Nothing, unusual." "Do you be- lieve in smoking or drinking? " " Nat to excess." " Do you believe in training ? " " Yes ;it is a good thing for anybody who has the time. I have not. Ido not train—that is, I have very seldom trained." " Would : you, do you think have done better if you had trained?" "Certainly." Looking at Mr. Shorland's light wiry frame and rather spare features, I could not help think- ing that he had no surplus flesh or fat to lose, and there- fore could very well do without training. He stands sft. Ilin., and has ridden a 58in. bicycle. " But lam heavier than you think. Guess my weight." I hazarded list., but was three-quarters short. " There is one thing boys would be interested to learn," I added, "Can you tell them what you think about when you are going round and round the track for twenty-four hours ? " "Business," promptly responded the English champion; and it may be explained that he is a prominent member of a well-known cycling firm. "It is just the same when Igo home and when Igo to bed. I always think about business before I drop off to sleep."

MR. EDISON'S LATEST INVENTION.

CONTINUOUS PHOTOGRAPHY.

NEW YORK, WEDNESDAY.-Mr. Edison has perfected his kinetescope for the photograph of objects in motion. The main principle of the invention consists of taking a great number of impressions by means of a camera in a very limited space of time, thus obtaining a continuous photograph of the entire motion of the object or person selected. The photographs follow each other in such rapid succession that no lapse of time can be detected between the impressions recorded, and the series of pictures becomes in effect but one picture. The subject chosen for the first photograph was the strong man, Sandow.

BY \ln. IT. WICTITAM.

I gather fc.om a short criticism that recently r-p- -peared in the Examiner, the possibility of my being somewhat misunderstood. If we should admit the right of the poor man to demand relief from •the rich, we should by that admission allow the right of one man to hold the possession of more than it suffices tor his necessities. To use the words bare subsistence" is unfair to the -Socialist,as they repre- sent a state ot things against which he is fighting. Now what are a man's necessities ? I suppose it will benllowed, by even the most thoughtless of individua- lists, shelter, food, and clothing, take a primary position, and that we shall be on common ground if we say that, in degree, all beyond these are luxuries. It is apparent here that in the common acceptance of the term, "a living wage" that the bared shelter, food, and clothing are to be the share of the workman, and that his share of the luxuries of life are to be nil.

At this stage of the discussion of the social question it is idle to draw comparisons between the workmen's condition to-day, and his condition say—in the days when George 111. was King. To do so would be to ac- knowledge at once that civilisation was a failure, and I for one am not prepared to do this. We are moving on- ward, and upward, we must all feel, that the trouble is that our moving is not done as a harmonious whole. Tennyson scored some of us pretty hard when he wrote— __

" lie that roars for liberty, Faster binds a tyrant's power; And the tyrant's cruel glee Forces on the freer hour."

===== 0002601_18940310_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
Some of us are roaring and pretty loudly for liberty. One of these days we shall be pulled up sharp to find ourselves somewhat like the American people are at the preseut time, blindly asking one another the meaning of this same liberty. A goodly number of us have been " Truthful, trustful looking upward to the practised hustings-liar," we may do so no longer, for we are perceiving that it is only through pure Socialism that true individualism can be gained and so liberty. Hamil- toa wrote years ago ; " Give to a man the right over my subsistence, and he has power over my whole moral being."l thought of this passage the other day while read- ing a Warrington friend's letter in which a description was given me of the drunken orgies the dear old town had gone.through,the past Christmas. Can we wonder at it ? Has not the system under which we live crushed true individualism, and set un an individualism that is false? One part of the community has been put upon the wrong road, and its personality has been absorbed by the other part. What we have left is mere dregs made up of the worst passions that cur natures have absorbed since we threw off the yolk of the middle ages. During the lock- out now happily through with, it remained for one man, the Rev. Stopford A. Brooke to voice the true position of the collier and of labour. He says :—" On the part of the men, self-sacrifice is pre-eminent. Each of them is suffering with a noble endurance for the sake of their whole class, not for his own sake alone. They have given up their money, their goods, their very susten- ance, for the love of one another ; and their women have, under the terrible trial of their starving children, supported the men. The ground of their action, of their refusal to work, is love of one another." ,This has been the whole history of trade-unionism. Self-sacrifice, often by very objectionable ways, but always with a principle founded on love of one another. The world will some day realise what it owes to the steady support of this principle by the workingmen of England. The rev, gentleman goes on to say :—" The maintenance of the idea of competition (every man for himself and the devil take the hindmost) has been of the plainest disad- vantage to the whole ccm infinity of men, and ,unchecked hr other tendencies, would finally, after a successive degradation, destroy mankind." Those who demur to this statement must remember that the Socialists posi- tion is not one of opposition to a healthy competition. , He is on the side of everything that will bring out the ' best in man, and the best in nature, but he sees plainly that a systeal of working the collieries which is based en cempetition and which finds it impossible to pay even a living wage must be rotten and corrupt at the core. Does my critic realize this, and is he prepared to answer it. I say the condition of workingmen has improved. It would be strange if it had not. He and his employer are the outcome of the era of steam, and to each have the words of the poet, as quoted,a fitful application. Labour has fought for freedom to live, for freedom to educate itself, for escape from slavery. Because the conditions of the workman have improved, because he has gained a higher view of life, his needs have increased, these needs will develop, and the more they develop the higher will be his demands. A higher and a nobler life demands more than a living wage. It calls for all that goes to- wards making life decent, to be able to be men, and to secure happiness for women. This principle once ac- cepted will, as Mr. Brooke says "run like a fire into every trade, and especially into those where the workers suffer most from the conditions of their labor." "Com- petition as a basis for the work of a State is played out, and the sooner we find in collectiveism or Socialism a fresh basis, the better for all mankind. In the United States more than in any other country competition is narrowing itself. Individuals are ceasing to compete wtth each other. Bad as the old system was, it is possible that the new era of companies, corners, and syndicates which now obtains may be worse. The small tradesman is being lost in the store that covers a block. Se it is with the small manufacturers—all are being swallowed up in the maw of self. When I quoted, , recently, the golden rule as a porticn of the ethical law running through the New Testament, I was quietly re- ferred to the so-called Pagan writers for the original reading. This is quite true, but will my critic bear with me, if I tell him that it is of far more importance to get the rule accepted than it is to know who originated it. It may be that I have a right to look upon a cabin'd, cribb'd, and confined room in a workhouse as mine by right, because of my having been a taxpayer, and yet, strange to say, I would, if possible, avoid its pleasures. In all the arguments bi oug,ht to bear upon Socialism there is something artificial, and wanting in sincerity. This is to be deplored because the acknowledgment comes to us from the four corners of the earth that " we are all Socialists now." We all recognise with Aristetle that" A free man is one who belongs to himself, and to ' • other,"and we say with Mare, " that you must either :Al& the workpeople, or feed them." Is it possible to fit:d a workman so free that he dares have a personality ea his own ? The present writer has just been discharg- ed from the little employment he had, because he dared speak at an unemployed meeting from a Socialistic point of view. There is to-day such a state of terrorism throughout the industrial world as compels tte thought- ful reading workman to hide his talents. He knows full well if he does not, the employer, and the press will, hound him to death. In all my study of the problem of sociolcgy,l have found not one question raised by the masses but what has been accepted sooner or later by the classes. If we look at it historicaly we find that our more recent lawmakers have been engaged, as Mr. Gladstone himself confessed, in rescinding class made laws to make room for something more human. If history, not that of King's. Queen's and wars, but history that treats of the people's progress during the past century could be taught in our schools our eyes would be opened. We should be brought to see what the struggle between the town and country meant fifty years ago. How it was that out of apparently so innocent a question as the struggle between Protection, and Free Trade arose the Manchester school, which in its turn was the parent of the greatest curse that ever afflicted the people,—the factory system, John Bright as a shining light of this school was only consistent when he insisted upon the right of women, and children to work all hours, day or night. We are only new beginn- ing to see the fearful and direful force that was behind robbing the poor creatures of their "Freedom of con- tract." From the market place issued the cry for education. Some little help it had from above, and now we can read, as I have just read in the" Examiner's" report of a lecture delivered at Runcorn that "crime has fallen something like fifty per cent. during the last half century,and that there were several of her Majesty's gaols on sale at the present ;time, and if things went on in the same way we should not require Kautsford many more years." Should an Ameriean chance to read this he would readily account for it. I shall have further use for this excellent lecture. , If the masses have, through education, become more I honest, can we say the same of these who sit in the high , places? From the days of the Glasgow bank failure down to the recent collapse of the Liberation Society historical research would give us startling evidence that ! if there is any portion of the social organism that would ; be found wanting in any attempt at Socialism it would be found in the Socialists upper circles. In my attentpt to carry the war into the enenties camp I deal with only t published facts. These prove that fact is not only stronger, but stronger than fiction. "Thou shalt not steal" nor, as Carlyle said, "Shalt thou be stolen from" When we recognise that honesty, as a word, conveys the same impression in the office it is forced to assume in the workshop, we shall find that the poor do not need to" demand relief from the rich." Sir James Kitson read the following letter at Pittsburgh Oct. 9. 1890 on the occasion of the visit of the Iron and Steel Institute

of Great Britain to America. His argument was to prove that under a free-trade system the manufacturer could always hope to make higher profits titan under a protective system, because they were independent of the workman's vote. It is a letter written by Sir Henry Bessemer and it will be found in volume 2 (1890) of the transactions of the institute ; on page 309. Some idea may be formed of its importance as a manufacture when I state the simple fact that on the expiration of the four teen years term of partnership of our Sheffield firm the works, which had been greatly increased from time to time entirely ow, of revenue, were sold by private con- tract for exactly twenty-four times the amount of the whole subscribed capital of the firm, notwithstanding that we had divided in profits during the partnership a sum equal to fifty-seven times the gross capital,-so that by the mere commercial working of the process, apart from the patent, each of the firm partners retired after fourteen years from the Sheffield works with eighty-one times his subscribed capital, or an average of nearly cent per cent every two months, a result probably un- precedented in the annals of commerce.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
ONCE A MISERABLE MAN.

One day last summer I was a passenger on an express train from Liverpool to London. Halfway between two stations something broke down about the engine, and there we stuck and stayed until another engine was fetched to take its place. Then we rattled aleng lively enough, and got in forty minutes late. Some of the passengers growled and grumbled,and said the company ought to build engines that couldn't break down. We were delayed a bit yet nobody suffered on that account. But if the passengers had been vitally connected with the engine, then you know! What do I mean by that? I'll show you in a twink- ling. We get at it this way. We quote from a man who says: " felt that I qras the most miserable man on the face of the earth." What ailed him? Merely something wrong; not with Aim, essentially or in spirit, but with his machinery.

He goes on to say that in October, 1884, he had an attack of rheumatism. This left him weak and below par. He could eat but little, and that little seemed to be of no use to him.

To a sound man there is nothing like a hearty meal It drives away the blues, warms him up, and stands him on his legs every way. But every time our friend here swallowed anything, he paid for it by having Qains in the chest and sides and in the pit of the stomach. On his teeth and tongue a thick slime gathered,and he often vomited a green fluid, nasty and bitter. The secretion from the kidneys passed with pain and heat that amoun- ted to agcny, and was ropy and dark in colour. Something must have been very wrong indeed with the human wheels and levers t 3 make them go as badly as that, don't you think ? Yes; well, it was to be worse before better. On several occasions he passed small gallstones, which tor- ' tured him fearfully. . . .

These stones were created by the union of cert amn bile acids with the alkali of the body, and indigestion was responsible for the whole affair. If the liver hadn't been so sleepy and stupid, the bile acids wouldn'c have remained to do that bit of mischief. Make a note of it for you-self.

By-and-by asthma showed itself. Naturally enough, oh, dear yes! for how should the blood be full of sto- mach poison and the nerves not be half paralysed by it? And asthma is a spasmodic disease of the nerves:; it hasn't anything to do with the substance of the lungs. Don't blunder on this point, no matter what some so- called doctors say.

Some nights Mr. Thomas Kenny (who is the man that went through all this) would have to getout of bed and throw open the windows so as to breathe the fresh air— he was so choked and suffocated.

Here are his very words, or a few of them : "I got so wretched and nervous I could barely lift my food to my mouth. I had a strange trembling sensation all over me, and couldn't bear company or the slightest noise. I feared I should go out of my mind, and the doctors said if a change did not take place they would put me in the asylum." Just try to take that in; to grasp the idea. Great Powers of Mercy! Put a man in a madhouse because he was ill andsthey couldn't cure him! Providence deliver us, if not from disease, at least from such doctors.

"I had given up work, of course," says Mr. Kenny, "and I had partially lost the use of my legs. Several times I fell down in the street and had to be helped home."

===== 0002601_18940310_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
We must condense the rest of the story. He saw doc- tor after doctor, spent seven weeks in Leeds Infirmary, and was tapped there, fifty-seven ounces of water being taken away from him. At last his friend John McDermott persuaded him tJ try Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup. In ten days he was relieved, and by continuing to take it, was presently cured and back at his work. Mr. Kenny is a sawyer by trade, and worked at Mr. Trenams' Saw Mills, Kirkgate, Leeds, for seventeen years.

The full history of this remarkable case is published in a book which will be sent on application. It is also de- clared before Mr. Alf. Cooke, ex-Mayor of Leeds, in legal form, and signed by Thomas Kenny, 47, St. Mary's Street, Leeds. This breakdown of the vital machinery was due to a deep attack of indigestion and dyspepsia, and the cure by Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup adds one more to the long record of the triumphs won by that famous and popular medicine. Moral—A man is more than a passenger in his own body; he is closely bound up.with it ; so that when that breaks down he breaks down.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

OUTSPOKEN REMARKS BY REV. FATHER HENNELLEY.

At St. Mary's Roman Catholic Chapel, Latchford, on Sunday, the Rev. Father Hennellev referred the drunken- ness which prevailed in the neighbourhood, and strongly urged the congregation to avoid intemperance, not only because it was a sin in itself, but on account of the hor- rible sins of impurity which followed in its wake. He called attention to the condition of the inhabitants of a certain yard, nearly all of whom were supposod to be Catholics, and described the scenes which took place there at certain times. On one occasion he was struck by a missile which came through a window of one of the houses during the progress of a row. He had always observed that a man no matter how drunk he was,always showed some sign of respect for the priest, but he would defy any priest to go amongst the drunken women in that yard without being assailed by abominable foul words.

"INSPIRATION AND THE BIBLE !"

At Cairo-street Unitarian Chapel on Sunday evening, the Rev. W. B. Drummond preached in his "monthly talks with workingmen," on the subject of' Inspiration and the Bible." Alluding to the correspondence in the " Christian World "on " Is the Bible popular ?" he said he had come to the conclusion that in the sense of being carefully and thoughtfully read by the masses of the Bible was not strictly speaking a popular book. One reason for this was that it Was to a large extent a book set apart from all other books and not treated in the same way. One great cause of doubt as to its ver- bal inspiration was the great progress made in science, from which it was found that the Bible was not correct in its science. Another cause was the application of scientific truth to the study of history, by which it was found tnat many portions of the book w ere historically ' untrue. Its apologists teed in vain to harmonize science and the /Sible. The preacher said the Bible was not one book, but a collection of 66 books extending over many centuries, some of them recording events occurring cen- turies before the time of the writer. They were books recording the religious and national struggles of the Jewish people, And as such showed signs of progrss and development. Even its morality was not infal. Ile for that was progressive. Inspiration was not the ono- polv of the Bible, and was not closed with the book of Revelation. God was still influencing men, drawing them on to higher thoughts and nobler purposes.

THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

This P.S.A. society celebrated its first anniversary on Sunday afternoon. The Rev. J. Yong,,e presided, and gave a review of the first year's working of the society. He stated that during the twelve months 1152 persons joined the brotherhood. During the first half-year 392 prizes and 80 certificates were awarded, and during the past half year 600 had earned prizes. 51 persons had signed the temperance pledge, and the benevolent fund had amounted to £3B 6s. 5d., which had enabled them to assist in 181 eases. Nearly 1100 visits had been paid by the Visiting Commitiee. There had been 36 sick cases and six deaths,one so recently as the previous day when a young man named Arthur Legge passed away. 42 members joined the Ambulance class, 19 of whom sat for examination, and all passed. He hoped there would be no striking off of names for their second year of the P.S.A., and he wanted the old members to become recruiting officers. If they went on as they had done they would become a moral power in the town.—Alder- man Peach of Stafford, said he was very pleased to be there that day in helping them to celebrate their first anniversary. It was very gratifying to him to seo such a large number of men present. He then delivered a striking address on " Personal Influence" and was fre- quently applauded. Mr. R. H. lem." Mr. W. H. Paytm presided at the organ. The meeting was also taken part in by Mr. Edwin Walling- ton and Mr. W. Hickman..

In the lower vestry of the church on Saturday evening the distribution of certificates to the members of the St. Jol,n Ambulance class took place. The Rev. J. Yonge presided. An interesting feature of the proceedings was the presentation by the members,of a smokers' com- panion to Dr. Braide, who has conducted the class, and also the presentation of a copper kettle to the secretary of the class, Mr. Buckley. The Rev. J. Yorige made the presentations. Dr. Braide in thanking the members for the gift said it was desirable that workingmen should know how to render first aid to the injured. M. Buck- ley also expressed his thanks. Mr. Malkin and Mr. Mills also spoke. BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHAPEL.

- - The borne missionary anniversary services were held at Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel, on Sunday, when the pulpit was occupied in the morning by the Rev. Owen Watkins, and in the evening by the Rev. G. D. Mason. On Wednesday evening, a public meeting was held in the chapel when the chair was occupied by Councillor A. Bennett, and the Rev. Charles Garrett, of Liverpool, attended as a deputation. There were also on the plat- form, the Revs. J. Kirtlan, Owen Watkins, G. D. Mason, and J. G. Bennett. There was an excellent audience. After singing and prayer. the Rev. Owen Watkins read the annual report. He said that the income of the society during the past year had been £35,092 15s. 91., and the expenditure £34,554 95.. which after meeting an adverse balance of some £470 from the preceding year, left a balance of 67 9s. 4d. Towards this the Warrington circuit had contributed £7O us. 3d. The Chairman after apologising for the absence of his father, Mr. Wm. Bennett, who had not recovered from his recent illness, said that he was pleased to be present at that meeting. It was the first time that he had ever presided over a similar meeting at Bold-street. He had great sympathy with the movement as he believed it to be the work of a true Christian. He thought they ought to be ashamed (if themselves professing Christianity,and yet tolerate such deplorable spectacles as could be wit- nessed in their large towns. He was proud to think that they as Wesleyans were doing so much to benefit the heathen, both at home and abroad. Although he was not a very strong believer in making people good by Act of Parliament-, still,;he thought, that by well-considered legislation they might do much to take temptation out of the way of the people, and both parties might join in the work.

Miss Lawton next sang" It is well with my soul "the choir joining in the chorus. The Rev. Charles Garrett said that he was delighted to see Mr. Bennett in the chair, and also that he was in the Town Council. They wished to regenerate tite ToWn Council, and if they could regenerate the Town Council they would soon regenerate the town. They wanted in the Town Council and in the House of Commons men who would vote as they prayed. Mr. Garrett then pro- ceeded to give an account of his mission work in Liver- pool,and also of the overthrow of the drink party in that •city, showing the marvellous results which had followed in the wake of the victory of the temperance party. The Rev. J. G. Bennett followed with an interesting account of his work in various parts of England as a connextional evangelist. At the close of the meeting a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the chairman and the Rev. Charles Gar- rett and J. G. Bennett, on the proposition of the Rev. Owen Watkins.

WARRINGTON BOARD uF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday. There were present :—Messrs. G. Ratcliffe (in the chair), W. Houghton (junior vice- chairman), Muir, Pilling, Dotson, A. J. Jolley, A. Jolley, Hill, Aukland, Shaw, Longton, Dignum, Fairclough, Tartan, R. Owen, junior, the Rev. G. F. Roberts, and Messrs. J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (deputy clerk), C. H. Ward (master), and H. M. Mines (superintendent of the schools).

===== 0002601_18940310_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
PADGATFI SCHOOLS RETURNS.

Mr. Loncrro-ii-iaid that some time ago Mr. Alfred Jolley Asked for a return of the expenditure on the Pad. gate Schools. He wished to know when that return would be submitted. He noticed that day that there was an item of £B4 ss. 6d. in connection with the same schools.—The CLERK said that the return would be sub- mitted along with that for the Workhouse at the next meeting of the Board when the report of the estimates would be submitted.—Mr. LONGTON expressed himseif satisfied.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
OUTSTANDING CALLS.

The CLIMB said that there was still one call whi-h had not been sent in by the Townships of Penketh, Win. wick, and Rixton. He must press tor payment as they would encounter some difficulty in getting through the half year without the assistance of the outstanding calls. It was competent to call upon parishes at any time. Of course they could meet the difficulty by de ferring payments, but he wished the overseers to under stand that a very early call would be required in the next half year, otherwise the Union would he without- funds. They could as a Board insist upon special rate if necessary.—The CHAIRMAN said that those townships in arrears ought to be called upon to pay promptly. It was unfair to the townships which had paid, for others to be in arrears.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
INSURANCE OF UNION BUILDINGS.

Mr. SHAW said that some time ago he had suggested that the insurance of the Union buildings should be done through one of their own officials, so that the Union might derive the benefit of the 15 per cent. which the agent generally received. He therefore proposed that their Clerk be asked to write and apply for the appoint- ment, and that he carry on the work. The Warrington Corporation insured their property through the borough treasurer, and he had been offered terms on those coed:-

The Clerk stated that he had received the following letters with respect to the proposed closing of Bruns- wick and Granville-rt. schools. Mr. Simpson wrote to the Chairman of the Board on the 17th February as fol- lows .—" I beg to state that the managers cif toe Wes- leyan Day Schools have now definitely decided to close the schools,or to transfer them to the School Board after the next annual inspection in July. I am writing to the Education Department, informing them of this deci- sion." On March sth, Mr. Simpson wrote again : —" I beg to enclose papers I have received from the Education Department, relating to the transfer of the Wesleyan Day Schools to the Board. I have kept a duplicate set of the documents.' Mr. J. W. J. Stephenson, of the Education Department, in a letter dated 28th Feuruary, said : "I am directed to enclose for your consideration the papers usually sup- plied by this Department, when it is proposed to trans- fer a EL:II(NA to a School Board under section 23 cf the Elementary Education Act, 1870. These documents, to- gether with the present letter, slionld be placed in the hands of the Clerk to the School Board. If the Board and the parties concerned desire to effect a transfer of the school in question under section 23, the clerk to the Board should comply with the instructions contained in the " 2 T."—The Clerk reinarked that the papers en- closed in the letter from the Educational Department were necessary forms to be filled up if the transfer was decided upon. He fur ther explained that clause 23 pro- vided for tue traneter from managers of voluntary schools to the Board School in cases where the premises were held in trust for educational purposes.—The Chair- man said they were scarcely in a position to deal with the question at that juncture. He express et his regret that the managers of the IVesleyan schools :MOST have come to such adetermination,and should have resolv- ed to discontinue the educational work which they had so successfully conducted for many years. The obligation was thrown upon them by the closing ef the schools ot providing instruction for about 700 children, arid thus the responsibility of the members would be in that de- gree increased. They had no information before them as to the tenancy of the school whether thay were priv- ate institutions or in trust for educational purposes. If they were the former he understood they would be able to rent them on terms agreed upon, but if they were in trust it would be necessary to get the sanction of the educational department for their transfer. This being the case consideratle time would elapse before the Board would be a position to make final arrangements for the transfer of the schools. They were within three or four months of the closing of the school year, and there- fore he thought it would be necessary for them to make some provision for the continuation of the work, and to arrange for the temporary transfer of the schools to the Board. Before they could go further it would be wise to ascertain all the inormation they possibly could as to the terms upon which the school' could be rented to the Board. He moved that the Clerk write to the managers to obtain those particulars as well as to the conditions under which the institutions would be transferred to them.— Dr. McDougall in seconding the motion said lie thought it would be wise to discuss the subject in camera. It would be best if the clerk acknowledged the receipt of Mr. Simpson's letter and invited a reply as to the terms upon a hich tke managers were prepared to rent the schools to the Board for a fixed period. The question of the future policy of the Board might be discussed here- after. When they received the information the subject could be thoroughly gone into by the Board in commit- tee, and ultimately they could formulate a scheme for the future.—The Chairman said it would be necessary to discuss a number of details and these could be best done in committee. Mr. Handley coincided with the expressions of opinion as to the subject being discussed in camera. He could not see but that they would be obliged to temporarily rent the schools. In the meantime the members could look round and consider the question of transfer.— After a somewhat lengthy discussion, it was unani- mously resolved that the Clerk write to Mr. Simpson to ascertain all possible information as to temporary rental as well as permanent transfer. On receipt of the parti- ctlars in question he was desired to communicate with the members of the Beard, so that in committee the whole question might be discussed.—This terminated the business.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

On Tuesday evening the monthly meeting of the Runcorn School Board took place in the Town Hall, Runcorn, Mr. Pritchard occupying the chair. There were also present :—Dr. McDougall,Canon A. Mait- land Wood, and Messrs. E. Imison, S. Taylor, T. Handley, and George Moss, (clerk.)

===== 0002601_18940310_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE.

The Clerk read the minutes of this Committee meet- ing on the 13th February, frem which it appeared that out of 37 parents summoned 34 attended. It was resolved that proceedings should be taken against 13 parents. At the meeting of the Committee on the 27th Feb., 30 parents appeared out of 33 summoned. Pro- ceedings were decided to be taken against eight persons. It was recommended that Mr. J. Yates tender, to make visitors uniforms for I:2 10s. 6d. be accepted. The at- tendance returns for February showed children on the rolls 3,559, against 3,751 in February last; the falling off of 192 being attributed to families having left the town. The average attendance was 2,982, against 3,029 ; a de- crease of only 67, the attendance having been more regu- lar than in February last year. The percentage was 832 against 807. The minutes were adopted on the motioq of Mr. Handley.
