The political THE great event in the world of . Situation. politics about which there was so much 'l..eliminary talk last week is now an accomplished fact. Mr. GLADSTONE has res_ igned his position as Prime Minister and as leaut_T of the Liberal Party, and he has been sue-

===== 0002601_18940310_art0060.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
-

===== 0002601_18940310_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
• ceeded by Lord ROSEBEE.Y. The full significance of this momentous change is not yet realised, and its results can not be predicted with anything like certainty. The greatest of English Parliamen- tarians and the greatest reforming statesman of this century disappears from a stage on which he has figured for sixty years, and during a consider- able part of that period as by far the chief actor* It is, in some respects, a pathetic incident, and there are multitudes of Englishmen who regret that it has taken place, though in the very nature of things Mr.. GLADSTO.NE'S retirement from the strenuous public life that he has led could not have been long deferred. For the Liberal party and the cause they represent, the event is of the' deepest import. It is true, as the National Liberal Federation says in its manifesto, that "principles do not perish with persons, that how- ever great the worker, the work is greater, and that the cause remains though the leader is changed"; but at the same time it would be fatu- ous to overlook how much depends upon the tact land capacity of a leader in the practical working lof our party system. Of Lord ROSEBERY there is yet something to be learned. In describing him •as a "peer in a poke," Mr. LesoucHsste only once . again shows that he is fonder of an epigram than ,of a plain and accurate statement of the case. At I the same time it must be admitted that during the last two or three years Lord ROSEBERY has been I the most reticent of our statesmen. 'What he has said, however, all goes to show that in . naming him as his successor Mr. GLADSTONE has made the wisest selection he could have made It may be said that the future of I the Liberal Party is, above all else, bound up I with the cause of social reform, and in this res- pect Lord ROSEBERY has a very promising record. He has as chairman for some time and later en as a private member, taken a prominent part in the work of . that most progressive of all our local governing bodies, the London County Council,and his influence has always been used in the further- ance of an enlightened policy in municipal affairs.. There is every reason to believe that he will catry the same spirit and method into national affairs. . That he understands and sympathises with the demands of the democracy is attested by a speech which he delivered in May, 1892, in Edinburgh. Referring upon' that occasion to the great Liberal measures which have been forced to the front by the millions who were enfranchised in 1884 and 1885, he used some significant language. "These now constituents," he said, " have been formula- lating these new wishes and these new demands, and they say to you now, in accents which you cannot mistake; We will no longer he satisfied with the crumbs that fall from your table ; we demand an equal seat at the board.' In my . opinion, that is a righteous and a justifiable wish. They say, You have long legislated for one class or other, whichever class at the time happened , to have political predominance.' And they say it decidedly now, You shall take our claims into consideration our claims to a more favourable share of the land, to better and more sanitary homes, and better air spaces, to shorter hours of labour, and, indeed, to a bet- ter share altogether of the amusements and the recreation of life, and these claims should be re- cognised in a practical manner by British Parlia- ments and British municipalities." Having re- peated these demands of the democracy, Lord ROSEBERY goes on to uphold them, and to argue that a government which springs from the people should be a government by the people and for the people. There •is no reason to believe that Lord ROSEBERY is now less disposed to follow out the lines of this speech in regard to so cial legislation than he was in 1802. If there has been any change it is probably in the direction of even a fuller' sym- pathy with the demands of the w orking classes. That the new Premier is not believed to be either weak or lukewarm in regard to any other of. the reforms initiated by Mr. GLAD - STONE is evidenced by the singular unanimity and enthusiasm with which all sections of the Liberal party have rallied around him. Even Mr. LABOUMIERE and the two or three other Radicals who were up in arms against a " Peer-Premier" have now laid aside their hostility. The conten- tion that a capable statesman should be ostracised from any office because he had the misfortune to be born a Peer is not good Radicalism. Real Radical policy is to en franchise not to disfran- chise. All that can be fairly asked of Lord Rose- bery is that Ihe shall be determined like other Liberals to annul or restrict within narrow limits the veto which the House of Lords now exercises over Liberal legislation, and it is understood that on this score his policy will leave nothing further to be desired.

Now that the Parish Comtcils Bill A 61" t Reform, has passed both Houses of Parlia- ment a widespread interest is being felt as to its probable effects. The shadow of the great reform will be over all the vestry and town- chip meetings that are to be held shortly. Before they can be held another year, the parish and dis- trict councils which will be created by the Bill will have taken in hand the government of rural dis- tricts. Boards of Guardians will undergo a note- worthy change. They will become the district councils for the rural parishis of the Union, and they will be elected on the one man one vote prin- ciple and not upon the property franchise that now I exists. After November next it will be as easy for the working classes to elect one of their own rank to the Board of Guardians as it is now to the Town Council. The Boards of Commissioners at Run- corn and Newton and the Local Boards at Lymm and Haydoek will become district councils.and all the "fancy franchises" upon which members of these bodies are elected will be swept away in favour of the one man one vote. The chairman of each district council will, by virtue of his office, be entitled to sit on the local bench of magistrates, just as the Mafor of a bomugh now is. It is, however, the rural parishes or townships that will ie most vitally affected by the new Bill. Hitherto they have, for t'Ae most part, been without any- thing worthy of the name of representative government. That want will be met by the parish councils, and it will be the villagers own fault if they do not make the best of the powers that have been conferred upon them. They will now be able to adopt, if they so desire, such Acts as those relating to Lighting and Watching, Baths and Wash-houses, Burials, Public Improvements, and Libraries,though the expenditure must not be More than sixpence in the pound in any one year. Then, in addition to all this, the Councils may either purchase or hire land and let it out in allotments. Altogether, the Act which comes into force. this year can hardly fail to ha ye some far-reaching effects.

MR. MACCABE'S VISIT TO. RUNCORN. This well-known entertainer paid a visit to Runcorn

_ . on 'Tuesday evening, and the varied programme pre- sented by him was very highly appreciated by a fairly large audience. Mr. Maccabe has rightly termed his performance" Begone dull care," for scarcely a moment is allowed to elapse without the risible faculties of those present being brought into active exercise. Although he has been in the profession for such a lengthened period he shows no sign of lack of vigour or ability in the por- trayal of the phases of life of which he has made a life- long study. DEATH OF A RUNCORN LADY IN AUSTRALIA. Many of our readers will learn with regret that Mrs. Evans, late of High-st., Runcorn, died on the 28th Jan- uary, at the house of her son, Kirkham Evans, of Ade- laide, Australia. The news of her death was despatched to some Ituticorn friends at the end of January, and reached here in the middle of th e present week. MIT. Evans who was a native of Rune orn made a large circle of friends during her residence ir the town, being asso- ciated with au adult class at Camden school as a teacher and also serving in the capacity of agent to the IV,,:leyan General Assurance Society. Some ten years ago she left this town for Australia, and has since permanently resided there with her three sons. For some months previous to her death she had been in failing health and was at the age of 63 years when she died..

BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY, RUNCORN. At the weekly meeting held on Wednesday a very friendly yet animated discussion took place on " Gretna- ation," between the members of the above and the Ellesmere-st. Societies. The Ellesmereites,who were re- presented by Messrs. Gandy, Bentley,Scragg, and Jones vigorously championed the cause of cremation ; while the Brunswickites, represented by Messrs.Palin,Lawton, Bisbrown, and Millington stoutly supported the time- honoured custom of burial. At the close the visitors were cordially thanked for their kindness in introducing the debate, which besides being keenly enjoyed by those present, also gave them an opportunity of hearing the subject thoroughly thrashed out, and a hope vc as ex- prusel that the joint debates which had been so success- ful, and which by bringing the members of the different societies together was productive of so much good feel- ing and fellowship, would be continued in the future. There was a crowded attendance. Mr. T. Simister (the presidcnt) occupied dui? chair. Over 6,000 delicious loaves sold every week at COLLIBed.

BETHESDA LITERARY AND SOCIAL CLASS, RUNCORN. At the weekly meeting of this class on Tuesday even- ing the Rev. J. Simpson delivered an interesting address on "Dinah Morris," one of the characters in George Elliott's works "Adam Bede." He dealt with the subject in a manner which riveted the attention of the audience. As an introduction to the question he showed that the highly sensitive nature of woman combined with her depth of feeling, compared with that of man, tended to the adoption of earnest religious principles. The char- acter of Dinah as idealized by George Elliott led them to the contemplation of the existence of woman as a minis- tering angel, ever ready to place self aside and yield her- self as a sacrifice for others; and with such an amount of Christiaa love and sympathy within her that in her walk upon earth she irOglit be looked upon as a veritable saint. Her religion was not of that hard, cold, and relentless kind which rather repelled than invited, but it was full of brightness,warmtli,and.sunshine so that those with whom she came into contact were made better by her presence. The motive power of this noble life was briefly summed up in her intense love for God and man with an earnest endeavour to do all in her power for the alleviation of another's woes and to raise mankind from indifference into active enthusiasm.' . SCENE AT THE POLICE STATION.

John Price, wanted by the Widnes police for committing criminal assaults on two little girls at Widnes, last Sunday, was captured at Warrington on Wednesday night. Detective-sergeant Mona- ghan came across Price in Buttermarket-street, Warrington. He .accosted him, and the man gave the name of ". Williams," working at the forg, Warrington, for the past three weeks. Seeing that the man's dress tallied with the description of the IVidnes police, particularly as to wearing a clog on one foot and a shoe on the other, he was taken to the Police Station by the detective. Price afterwards admitted his iden— tity, and was handed over to the Widnes police. On Thursday. at the Widnes Petty Sessions, riiCe, who is about .35 years, and of no fixed abode. was charged with assaulting two little girls- named Violet Lee, aged five years and Lily Lee, aged thre:, years ,- daughters of Oliver Lee, hobby-horse owner, at present residing in Widnes. SUNDAY CLOSING MEETING AT WIDNES.

A public meeting was held in the Parochial Rooms, on Wednesday night, under the auspices of the uentral Association tor stopping the bale of intoxicating liquors on Sunday, and to advocate the neces-sity of closing public houses on Sunday_ There was a good attendance. Mr. Stuart Dear.,on B.L.,occupied the chair, and he was supported on the platform by the deputation Rev. Geo. Armitage, of Manchester, and Mr. It. Foulkes Griffiths,barrister-at-law,Loixion ; and also by the Revs. 0. Hughes, and W. E. Lead ; Councillors Owens and OD nnell ; and Messrs. D. Lewis and F. Hartland. The Bev. W. E. Lead opened with prayer. Letters were read from several ministers who regretted being unable to be present.—Mr. Stuart Deaeou in his opening ad- dress stated that although he was not a teetotaller he was greatly in favour of the Sunday closing of public-houses. (Applause.)—Mr. F. riartland proposed " That while many questions connected with the control of the drink traffic might advantageously be referred to the local au- thorities, the Sunday closing of public-houses, for which public opt won has long been ripe, can and ought to be dealt with by an imperial measure which would take effect unifcrinly and at once throughout the whole of England." He said he was greatly in favour of closing public-houses on Sunday. Ile then referred to the great number of people employed on the Sabbath in the drink trade, and hoped Sunday closing would soon be paced in the statute book of this country. (Applause.)— Mr. 'l'. Ormroyde in a few well chosen remarks seconded the resolution.—The Rev. G. Armitage, in supporting the resolution, said when we came to take the figures as to the necessity or not of Sunday °losing we find a great majority in favour of it—about 7tl 1. There were also a great maj,rity of publicans in favour of the movement as they thought it would be right to give a day of rest to their employees. He said wherever Sunday closing had been adopted it had proved a real sucsess in every case. Factory hands worked about 56 hours per week, whilst in the London public houses IE3 hours had to be worked and 108 in the country. Was it not a shame that young people should be fastened up behind the bar so long ? The resolution was carried imariimnusly. Councillor Owens proposed that copies of the resolution iassed that evening he sent to the Hous,of PArliampntaid the mem- ber for the %Vitines division', Mr. J. S. Gilliat. He (Mr. Owens) thought that if Sunday Closints was passed it , would prove it benefit to the whole cosntry.—Councillor ! O'Donnell in seconding the resolution, thought that Sun- I , day closing should come into force if only for the sake of publicans and their assistants.—Mr. It. Foulkes G riffiths in supporting the resolution said sonic people thought that they were trying to rob the poor man of his beer. It was not so,and if beer was required for Sunday it could ke gst on the Saturday night when the groceries and vegetables were got in. He then referred very folly to the Welsh Sunday closing and the Royal Commission appointed to I enquire into it. Mr. Griffiths was vociferously applauded lat the end of his address. The speech,though somewhat • leni;thy, was full of interest, and interspersed with hum- our.—The resolution was carried unanimously. —On the , motion of Mr. David Lewis and Mr. T. S. Swale a hearty vote of thanks was given to Mr. Stuart Deacon for pre- siding. Mr. Deacon replied.—The meeting terminated by the Bev. %V. E. Lead pronouncing the benedietion.

At he Warrington County Sesions on Wednes- day, -before Mr: J. White in Ow chair, and Mr. S. R. Armitage, James .Jacks.m, landlord of the Crown and Cushion, Penketh, was charged with being drunk- in-that public house on Feb. 9th: P.C. Thorl ey said that he was in War- rington-road, Penketh, when he saw two men who were drunk,go into the pu bile house. He went into the house and told the barmaid not to serve the men as they

- were the worse for drink.--At this stage of his evidence, the constable appeared to have forgotten his case and stood for some time completely at a loss what to say, much to the amusement of the Court. After a; time he went on to say that the defendant came

staggering—Torrd-to the parloar door. Mrs. Jackson went to him and pushed him back ita the par- lour again and said to him "Stop there, there's a con- stable in the house." Jackson however came staggering

Swift of St. Helens; who re- presented the defendant, the constable denied that he often bad lapses of memory. He also denied having gene again' to the house at a later hour the same night. Oh being asked what was upon it, the witness replied that he did not know. He contended that the con stable's evidence was not to be trusted as he had a lapse of memory over giving his evidence. The, defendant totally denied being drunk at all, and also asserted that the officer came twice to the house on the night in question. On the first occasion the officer never said anything what- ever about Jackkon, hut on the Fecon :1 visit which. was about eleven o'clock at night when Jackson was in bed, he said that he had reported him for drunksnness. Jack- son was then inforilned, and he came downstairs, and asked why he had not.said that he was dronitsia the first visit if he had been so. THE EFFECT OF THE PARISH COUNCILS BILL.

The Clerk produced the circular from the Local Gov- ernment Board, explaining the effect which the Local Government Act, 1894, had upon Boards of Guardians. —Mr. Brandreth stated that they had been elected three years, but the Local Government Act had upset that arran;lernent,and they were going to have a new system of business, and probably it would take the Guardians all their time to know !sow to work under the new Act. FINANCE.

Mr. Brandreth presented the report and estimate of the probable balance in favour of or against each town- ship in the Union at the end of the current half-year ending Lady Day 1894 and amount of contributions required trom the overseers of the respective townships 1 in the Union to meet the expenditure ot the ensuing half-year, Michaelmas, 1894. The basis of contributions on the rateable, value as per valuation list in force showed the total capital for the current half-year ending Lady Day ainou-nted to £8,937 and the net probable expendi- ture was estimated aL £5,378, a balance of £3,609 at Lady Day. Time probable expenditure for the half-year ending Michaelmas 1834 was: Common charges, £4,000; cm ate rate, £3,403; balance of contingency fund, £2,810; total, £10,213. Thus after deducting the pro- bable balance at Lady Day the amount required by con- tribution orders from the overseers was £6,604. The rateable value of the Union as per valuation lists in force was £224,764; ad increase of £1,072 during the year. A ' rate of 7J. ill the £ would produce £6,558, and it was recommended that a call for £6,604 be made upon the overseers. The foregoing calculations were subject to alteration which might beenade in valuation lists previ- ous to the contribution orders being signed, as there were several supplemental valuation lists, assessing the Manchester ship Canal, and other new and supplemental valuation lists to be received.—Mr. Brandreth proposed that the report and estimate be adopted and that the precepts be for a rate of 7,1. in the L. In answer to a 1 question he stated that the amount was the same as that I levied for the same quarter last year. —The motion was l seconded by Mr. Heywood and carried.

TRH PAVING OF A YARD AT TILE WORKISOIISR.-EXTRA..

ORDINARY ACTION OF THE BOARD.

The Clerk repotted the receipt of the Local Govern- ment Board's sanction to the proposal of the Guardians tw employ Messrs. Dunbar, Kelly, and Co., to pave the girl's yard at the Workhouse with granolithic flags, at a chargt:of 43. 9J. per square yard.—Mr. Heywood said this matter, he had learned, would cost something like £l5O, and he did not think in view of such a large sum, that the question had had sufficient consideration.—The Rev. J. W. N. Tanner said that the matter had been con- sidered by the House Committee in the proper form, and had been duly presented to the general Board for their dis- cussion. The general Board had approved of the recmn- mendation, and the matter was therefore at an end.— Mr. Lea pointed out that there was something like 100 tons of paving stones in the yard, which at between Bs. and 9s.per ton would realise almost the required amount. —Mr. Norman said that the Committee had courted the fullest consideration from the Board on the matter. It had therefore not been rushed through. The yard was in a very bad state, and it was important that the work should be executed as soon as possible. They had con- side red the advisability of doing the yard with blue tiles but he had come to the conclusion that it would be best and cheapest in the long run to call in Messrs. Dunbar, Kelly and Co., and have it done with granolithic.—Mr, Heywood thought th4t the amount of the total etipentli- ture had not been named either in committee or at the meeting of the Board. All that was mentioned was that the work-would cost 4s. 9J. per yard, and the number of yards was not spoken of.—Several members stated that the yard was in a most dangerous state and needed urgent repair.—Mr. Handley also spoke faN- ourably of the course adopted by the coaimittee, and thought that no objection should now be raised after the Board had approved of the decision of the committee.-- Mr. Taylor remarked that as the matter had passed their own Board ant: the proposal had been sanctioned by the 14,K:A G. vernment Board, it was now inopportune for Mr. Heywood to take the stand he had done with regard to this matter.—Mr. Heywood then proposed that the contract with Messrs. Dunbar, Kelly and Co. be not entered intl until that day fortnight, and gave notice of a uyation he intended to bring forward at the next metting, to rescind the contract--This proposal found a 'seconder and immediately afterwards a nether guardian said that he would give notice of a motion to accept the contract at their next meeting.—The Master of the House was called into the room and gave it as his opin- ion that the yard needed repair. —The discussion con- tinued for almost halt-an-hour in the course of which Mr. Heywood offered to withdraw his moticn if his seconder would allow him, but the seconder refused to withdraw.—Ultitnately it was proposed, seconded and carried, that the matter should he left to the decision of the Ckairman who decided to adjourn the matter until the next meeting, when Mr. Heywood had given notiet to rescind the contract. RESIGNATION OF DR. ROBINSON. .

The Medical Officer, Dr. ,Robinson, writing on March sth, stated that owing to continued ill-health and gradual weakness, he was compelled to tender his resig- nation of the office of Medical Officer to the Workhouse. He thanked the Board for the great kindness and cour- tesy they had always shown towards him, and asked them to take into consideration the alloNcance to him of a superannuation grant. He thbught he was the oldest officer on the Board, having served for thirty years as medical officer to the Workhouse.—The Clerk in answer to a queston said that the Board had the power to superannuate Dr. Robinson, who had just turned sixty years of age, and had, as stated in the letter, served the Board for thirty years as medical officer.—Mr. Wiliiams moved that the resignation of Dr. Robinson be accepted and also suggested the appointment of a committee to inquire into the qaestion of superannuation. After the services Dr. Robinson lied performed in the capacity of medical offiter he thought it was desirable that a super- annuation grant should be allowed him. The preposition was seconded, and the following gentlemen were elected a committee to deal with the subject, and report to the Board :—Messrs. Brandreth, Williams, Norman, Hand- ley, Mclntyre, and Knowles. FAZE IDUCATION. Mr. T. Lea said that with a view of preventing the frequent discussions that toak place at the Board meet- ings on the question of school tees, he moved "That a petition be forwarded to Parliament to the effect that this Board is of opinion that all public elementary schools should be free, and Boards of Guardians should not be called upon to pay fees as at present '•• and praying that Her Majesty's Giovernment will amend the law accord- ingly."—The Rev. I. W. /t•Tanner seconded,and it was decided that the memorial should be forwarded to Mr. :Brunner, M.P.., for presentation in the House of Com- mons.

INTERESTING CEREMONY AT THE WARRINGTON SCHOOL OF ART.

A VISIT FROM LORD CROSS.

A fund was sometime ago started for the purpose. of placing in the Warrington School of Art a bust of the late Lord Winmarleigh. Tht. work has been executed by Mr. Bromfield Adams,Loudon and bears a rmarkable likeness to the deceased gentleman at middle age. The movement was brought to a con- summation on Thursday, by the unveiling of the bust, which was performed by Lewd Cross before a large company. In addition to Lnei Crass there were present : Mr. John Bylands (chairman) Mr. F. Monks, C.C., Aldermen Francornb, Sutton, and Wright ; Ceun- cillors R. T. Faircloue,h, Beird, Hill, Muir, and Plump. ton ; the Rev. F. W. Willis (rector of Warrington), Canon Penrhyn, the Revs. E. C. E. Carleton, H. Y. Pigot, G. F. Roberts. Morley Steveneen, and E. J. Wil- cocks ; Mejor Pickmere, Captains Withington, Buckton, and Edelstein ; Mr. John Wilite. Mr. aed Miss Cencon, Mr. R. T. Lowe, Mr. and Mrs. Milling, Mr. C. S. Bur- gess, Mr. J. E. Davies, Mr. and Mrs. Madeley, Mr. and Mrs. and Miss Woods, Mr. Bromfield Adams, Mr. C.and the Misses White, Mr. and Mrs. D. W. Finney, Mr.H. B. and Miss White, Mr. and Mrs. Thompson, Miss Massey, Mr. John Hatton, Mr. and Mrs. J. Warburton, Mr. and Mrs. W. Gill, Mr. L. W. and Mrs. Reyn Ads, Mr. G. F. White.and others. Communications re,gretting unvoid- able absence were received from the Mayor (Alderman E. Greenall) Alderman Harrison, Mr. Jelin Crosfield, Mr. Joseph Davies, and Mr. Thomas Morris. Lord Cross upon being called upon to unveil the bust said he esteemed it a great honour to have been invited to unveil the bust of an old townsman and a dear and valued friend of his own—Colonel John Wilson Patten, the first and last Lerd Winmarleigh. He was a true Lan- cashire man and a thorough gentleman. (Hear, hear.) As to his public life during his forty-two years' represen- tation of the nerthern division 01 tits county (1832 to 1874), he represented every one of his constituents, no matter what their politics were. He was a follower of Sir Robert Peel, and was called upon for some time to perform the arduous duties of chairmen of Committees of the House of Commons, an office of no inconsiderable dignity, and one which required great judgment, tact, and temper. Very much against leis will, he undertook and with enormous success adminie'ered, the office of Treasurer of the Chancellor of the I):ishy of Lancashire, and much more unwillingly he on leetook tne more im- portant duties of Chief Secretary to the Lord Lientenant of Ireland. During the time he held that office, when Ireland was not in the same condition as it is now, but was still difficult enough to manaee,he set his lane deter- minedly against the jobbery and corruption lie found in that country. With regard to his military career, he joined and devoted himself to the 3el Royal Militia Regi- ment, was made colonel in 1842, aesd led the regiment to foreign service in 1855. He was honourably men- tioned by the Field Marshall for valuahle services. War- rington had reason to be proud of their fellow townsman. There were two incidents by which he would be remem- bered by thousands and tens of thousands of his fellow- countrymen. One was the zeal and energy he threw into the work in 1862, and the manner in which he administered the cotton famine funds. With the excep- tion of Lord Derby, who headed that movement, there was no one who devoted so much time, afteation, and care as Lord Winmarleigh, then Colonel WilsorePatten, for the benefit of the cotton opera -ivee woo were suffer- ing at that time. The other incident was the very active part he took in starting, furthering, and promot- ing the welfare of the Northern [die': Asylum in Man- chester, an institution which had dons mach good there and elsewhere. Other matters could be enlarged uaon,, but it would suffice to refer to the pleasing actien of Lord Winmarlei,glnresiding amongst his own poopleeand showing what an English gentleman could de. 0 con- clusion as he withdrew the covering from the bast,Lord , Cross said Lord Winmarleigh U--eecil V. a, good old ages ' and died full of years, ric.nes,and honour, a man grontly 1)eloy0: tho Vist so 'ht all who ran tritht! ria I—read the ofaitiP or A oble and learn from him how they might do their duty in that state of life in which they might be called as he had done in his.—His Lordship then formally unveiled the bust, after which Mr. F. Moses, C.C., in accepting the bust on behalf of the Corporation, regretted that the Mayor oould not be present to perform the duty. The bust, he said, had been executed so as to resemble the deceased nobleman, not as he was in his old age, but as he was when he lived among them, and took an active part in their town's welfare. Referring briefly to Lard Winmarlergh's connection with Warrington, Mr. Monks said he remem- bered the active and energetic part which Lord Winmar- leigh played in connection with the Mechanics' Institute. In almost all other institutions for the benefit of the townspeople he was deeply interesteel, and helped both with his purse and with his personal services. (Ap- plause.) And when he left the town he made his name memorable by the generous manner in which he enabled Warrington to become possessed of what he considered their noblest buildine—(applause)—as well as the beautiful surroundings which formed an accept- able breathing spot for the inhabitants. WEEKLY ItEPOET ON TUE REALTII OP

WARRINGTON.

During the week, which was the 9th of. the current year, the following births and deaths were registered :—Births: Warrington, 32; Latchford, 1 ; t0ta1,33. Deaths: Warringioon. 19; Latchford, 2; total 21. The excess of births over deaths was 12. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 19.6 per 1,000, against 19.4 the average of the previous four weeks, and against 27.3 the corresponding week of last year. The death-rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 5.5 per 1,000, against 2.5 the average rate of the four previous weeks, and against 2 7 the corresponding 'week of 1893. The ages of mortality were :-0 to 1, 6 ; 1 to 5, 4 ; 5 to 15,0 ; 15 to 25. 1 ; 25 to 60, 8 ; 60 up 2: total, 21. The deaths included-2 from whoop- ing cough ; 1 from measles; 3 from enteric fever; 1 from cancer; 6 from brain disease ; 2 from lung disease ; 2 from heart disease. The following cases of infectious diseases have been reported during the week :—Scarlet fever, 2 cases. Total number of patients in hospital week ending 3rd March. 1894, 24. METEOROLOGY.—The mean temperature was 43 Fah., and therefore 7i deg. higher than the preced- ing week. The maximum temperature 54 Ir., was recorded on the 26th inst.. and the minimum temperature 33 F., on the 25th inst. The rainfall measared 1.13 inch. The general direction was s.w., the reading of the Anemometer denoting 475 miles.—JOHN H. GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

WARRINTON AMATEUR PHOTOGAPHIO SOCIETY. A special meeting of this Society was held ió the Committee room of the ittseum, Bold-street, on Tuesday evening, and was presided over by Mr. B. W. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

SERIOUS ASSAULT ON A WIFE.

At Warrington Police Court, on Saturday, John Wm. Pritchard, 30, Hamilton-street, was charged with an aggravated assault on his wife the previous Saturday afternoon. Mr. J. Lyon Whittle peosecuted and Mr. A. Browne defended the prisoner. Mr. Whittle said on Saturday the 24th February, the prisoner went home and gave his wife 15s. She remonstrated with him and told him that he only gave her 2e. the precious week. His reply was that that was all she would get from him,and he thereupon assaulted her. He knocked her down,pulled her by the hair, and stood upon her, and owing to her being in a pregnant condition at the time, did her grievous harm.—Mrs. Pritchard, wife of the prisoner, _gave evidence of the assault and admitted throwing two stones at the prisoner.—Mr. Browne, for the defence, spoke of the prisoner's previous gGod character, of the complainant's aggravation of him by her habits, and his intention to separate from her.—Dr. Jack gave evidence of the wounds, which in his opinion were the result of kicking. The magistrates committed the prisoner to gaol for 14 days' hard labour, and granted a separation order, the prisoner to contribute 15s. per week towards his wife's and childrens' maintenance.

ARRESTING A MAN WITHOUT A WARRANT. At the Warrington Borough Court, on Saturday morn- ing; Henry Miller was charg, d with neglecting to main- tain his wife. Mr. Basil Shearer prosecuted on behalf. of the Warrington Guardians, and Mr. A. Browne repre- sented the defendant. Mr. Browne asked for the warrant—Mr. Shearer said there had been no warrant taken out in that case. It had been customary when they thought a man was likely to leave the town to apprehend him at once without a warrant. The pris- oner was apprehended the previous night.—Mr.Brwne referred to a case, Honey v. Rogers, in which it was stated that a constable was not justified in apprehend- , ing a man on such a charge without having a warrant in his possession. The defendant was therefore illegally in custody, and the Guardians were responsible for damages. The magistrates dismissed the prisoner. Mr. Shearer then handed a warrant to the magistrates to be signed and this having been done, Inspector Bowen read it over to Mr. Miller who had not left the box, and the case was proceeded with. Mr. Shearer stated that the prisoner had neglected to maintain his wife and child for three weeks, and she had had to go to her relatives. He had left his employment and had Fold the furnil:ure, and had about 210 in hit; possession. Mr. Browne Con- tended that his client had no intention of running away and had not had an opportunity of seeing his wife since he received the results of the sale. An amicable arra/we merit was afterwards come to between the two parties and Mr. Shearer asked to withdraw the case. In dis- missing the case, the Chairman (Mr. John Burgess) said he thought no exception should he trade in future with regard to persons arrested on behalf of the Guardians. He was of the opinion that the prisoner ought not to --tave been locked up.

MINOR CASES.

WARRINGTON BOROUGH POLICE COURT. MONDAY.—Before Mr. F. Monks (chairman) Aldermen Harrison and Francomb, and Messrs. J. W. Hayward and S. Wright, A SAD CASE.—Kate Devany, a girl only twelve years of age, was charged with stealing from the stall of Joseph Frisby, or knowing to have teen stolen, a pair of boots valued 3s. 6d. From the Chief fonstable's state- ment it appeared that the girl went into the " Tower " to pledge the boots, and on being questioned by Mr. Dalton, the manager, she ran away. Mr. Dalton fol- lowed her and caught her in Mer Fey-street. A constable .14-as sent for, and she then said that an old woman in Scotland-road had sent her to the shop to pledge than. P.C. Corcoran took her to the police station and then went out to find the owner of the boots. They were identified by Mr. Isaac Bowers, manager for Mr. Frisby. The girl was then charged with stealing or receiving the boots in question, knowing them to have been stolen,but she denied the charge.—lsaac Bowers. Mr. Dalton, and P.C.Corcoran gave evidence in corroboration. P.S.Row- bottom said that he had visited the house of the girl's father, and found that her mother had left home some years ago, and was living with another man. The girl was very much neglected, and she was in a filthy condi- tion when caught on Saturday night. Her father also was cohabiting with a woman.—Th, magistrates were of opinion that the girl should be sent to some home as there was evidently nothing but ruin before her if she remained where she at present was. It being necessary to ask the father's permission. the magistrates asked the father if he was willing to have his daughter sent to a home where she could be cared for, but he said he was not. After a short consultation, Mr. Monks said tie magistrates had decided to give the girl the benefit of the doubt, but if there had been any possibility of tak- ing her out of her father's hands, they would gladly have done so, as they were convinced that the girl would be ruined. He could promise her father that the police would have their eyes upon him.—The father hereupon promised that he would send the girl to a friend of his. TEmponAny AUTHORITY.—WiIIiam Green was granted temporary auttiortty to sell in-- the " Shakespeare," in lieu of Mrs. Young. TIIESDAY.—Before Mr.E.J.Guest (chairman), and Mr. J. H. Fleming. Tirana-N.—John Morris,an ironworker,living in Stan- ley-st., was charged with using threats to Mrs. Hart, in July 1892. Mr. P. Davies prosecuted. He stated that the complainant lived at 4, Stanley-st., near to the defendant's house. The threats were used during a dis- turbance at Friars Green in July 1892. The complainant was so afraid of the defendant that she then applied to the magistrates for a summons against him which was granted. The defendant, his wife, and family however left the town, and nothing was heard of him until the previous Saturday. Evidence as to the nature of the threats used were given by the complainant and her hus- band, and Mrs. Collins, 10, Stanley-St. The magistrates bound the defendant over to keep the peace for six months in a surety of £5, and to pay the costs, or in default to go to prison for fourteen days. ASSAULT.—James Carlisle, butcher, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Naylor-st., and further charged with assaulting Arthur Meakin. The complain- ant stated that he was going home the previous night, and when opposite Naylor-st., the defendant without any provocation struck him a blow in the mouth. P.C. Forster then came up, and the defendant in the officer's presence again struck Meakin. He was locked up. The magistrates fined the defendant 53. and costs, or seven 4,aya' imprisonment. The prisoner was arrested in Warrington the previous day by Inspector Spinks and D.S. Monaghan. The magistrates handed him over to the Durham police. ComMITTED FOR" ONE CLOWT."—Llenry Welsh was charged with having on the sth March assaulted and beaten Mary Welsh, his wife. The complainant stated that she was asleep upstairs on the night of the sth inst. when the prisoner came to her and asked her for a penny She refused to give him one, and he thereupon struck

her in the face causing her nose to bleed. He also pulled her by her hair and dragged her down. Sergeant Chant- lee gave evidence as to arresting the prisoner. In reply to the charge he said "I only gave her one clowt." He was committed to gaol for one month hard labour. TEMPORARY AnntortiTy.—Mr. Jenks, late occupier of the Green Man public house applied for temporary authority,to sell at the Albion Hotel, Battersby-lane. The prisoner attempted to enlist in the South Lancashire regiment when it was found that he was a deserter since 1832. He was sent to Strangeways gaol to await an escort. NEFARIOUS PERSONS.—Rose Ann Macdonald and Min- nie Hartley, pleaded guilty to stealing from the lodging- house of James Macdonald, 3, Hill-st.,a cotton sheet,and Hartley was further charged with stealing from the shop of George Jackson a woman's shawl value 6i. 6J. Thel chief conetable stated that Macdonald came from Liver- pool and Hartley belonged to Bedford Leigh. Both were married women living apart from their husbands, and came out of gaol on the 27th of Feb uary. Constable Barlow saw the prisoners going up Church-st. the pre- vious day and noticed that they were carrying something bulky. They stopped at Jackson's pawnshop; Macdon- ald staying outside and Hartley went to the backway entrance to the pawnshop. P.C. Barlow went to them and they told him that the sheet pawned was their own. They were brought to the police station, and charged with stealing it. Macdonald said Hartley had lent it her to pawn. Hartley however, said she had nothing to do with it. The other prisoner took it off the bed and gave it her to pawn. Macdonald then said to Hartley "What have you taken from that pawnshop and put under your clothes." The shawl produced was found on Hartley and Mr. Jackson identified the shawl as his pro- perty. The magistrates sent the prisoners to gaol for fourteen days hard labour each.—A lad 12 years of age named John Gamble was charged with, and pleaded guilty to, stealing from the works of Edward Connor, Golborne-st., a quantity of horse hair value sixpence. He was ordered to receive six strokes with the bireh rod

ALLEGED DESERTION.—Miranda Wilson, 33 West-st., summoned her husband, Christopher Wilson, 43 Chorley- st., charging him with deserting her on March 7th, and not contributing to her maintenance.—Mr. A. Browne defended.—The complainant alleged that she left her husband about the above date, and on going to the house again, her husband refused to maintain her, and threa- tened to break her fingers with the poker if she got any- thing to cat. She had been in the Workhouse for a fortnight, and her husband had promised to contribute , to her maintenance in that institution, but she did not wish to be in tbe Workhouse.—Mr. Browne contended that there could be no desertion, as the wife had left the husband. The magistrates were in a dilemma for some time, as they did not consider it right that the wife, should go the Workhouse, and Mr. White, the clerk, up- held Mr. Browne's contention. They ultimately dis- missed the case, and advised the wife to go to the house of her husband and demand shelter. WARRINGTON YOUNG WOMEN'S CHRISTIAN A SSOCIATION.

A SUCCESSFUL CONCERT.

There was a good audience at the amateur con- cert given under the auspices of the Warrington Young Women's Christian Association held in the. Co-operative Hall, Sankoy-st., on Tuesday evening. The Association has been in existence in Warrington for about four years, and meetings are held in the Young Men's Christian Association Rooms every Friday night. Miss Pierpoint is president of the Association,•and Miss B. Dutton is hon. sec. Thvoncert on Puesday evening was in aid of the Association, and also to make it more ' generally known. Tie chair was occupied by the Roy. Podmoro Clark, curate of Daresbury. The vocalists were Miss E. fluhi-Nowermbe, 4cliss Maddock, Mr. Field- house, and the Latchford Quartette party, and all sang in such good style as to secure hearty roceptioas. Miss Newcombe's two songs were " Casta Diva" (Norma),and "Lo hear the gentle Lark." Both songs were exceed- ingly well sung and the audience showed their apprecia- tion b; emphatic applause on each occasion. She com- plied with both recalls, and still further delighted the audience. " Lkimare " was Miss Maddock's host effort and she received a well deserved encore. Miss Maddock also sang with Mr.Fieldhouse in the duet "Noontide heat has long passed over." Both " The Star of the Desert " and " 'The Admiral's Boom," were well suited to Mr. Fieldhouse's style and he did full justice to them. The Latchford Quartette Party contributed three glees,and in the one "Johnny Schmoker " in response to an enthusias- tic recall thry sang "As the moments roll." A feature at this concert was the performance of Romberg's "Toy Symphony "by a number of instrumentalists under the conductorship of Mr. Atkin. A number of the instru- mentalists, if such they can be called, consisted of toys used by children. • The effec':, produced,however, was in no way inhartnonius, and received duo appreciation from the audience. Those who took part in it were the Misses Smith, the Misses F4ircluugh. the Misses Nunner- ley, Dr. Reg,. Smith, Captain Dutton Fairclough, Mr. Leonard Smith, and several others. The instrumental part of the programme was also well sustained by the contribution or two violin solos and a pianoforte solo. Miss Guest played " Air Melodieux "on the violin, and Miss Jobb Nurinerley also contributed a violin sole, for , which she received an encore. Miss Dutton received a , similar compliment for her pianoforte solo.

The CHAIRMAN during a short interval made a few remarks. He said the reason he had come was not be- cause lee belonged, or had any connection with the Association, but because Miss Dutton lived in hi 3 parish an was a personal friend of his, and when she called upon him to take the chair he was very glad that he had. a free evening. Miss Dutton was one et those who deserved all the support she could get. (Applause.) Therefore he was very pleased, to do what little he could to help her in her grand work in connection with that Association. From a conversation with Miss Dutton he understood that the chief object she had in view in get- tine up that concert was to make it an advertisement for the Young Women's Christian Association as well as to raise funds. By bringing together a number of men and women they were able to bring prominently before them the Association for their sympathy and their help. From a paper Miss Dutton gave him he found that there were in the Young Women's Christian Association 100,000 young women, scattered throughout the northern part of England. He also noticed that in connection with the Association in many of the large towns they had capital institutes, where they had various instruc- tion and bright and happy evenings. In Warrington so far they could not point to any building which bore the name of the Warrington Young Women's Christian Asso- ciation, but still great things had smell beginnings. Though that Association had been at work in Warring- ton for four years, there was no reason why in another four years they might not have their ewn building for their Association. (Applause.) He might just tell them what the object of the association was. In the old days people seldom left the town or village where they were brought up. It was rather a thing new for people to go beyond thePr own village or towra. The result was than people went north, south, east and west, where they were perfect strangers. A young woman, belonging to the Association, when leaving home for another town, went with a recommendation from the local secretary and thus was introduced to pleasant companions at the Association there. Not only did she :min friends but she was able to spend a pleasant evening- in instruction or recreation. Here in Warrington through the want of helpers Miss Dutton was only able to have one class a week, viz : A bible class one week, and a working class or social class the other week ; but if some ladies would come forward and help there was no reasen why they should not he held every night. (Applause.) The rev. gentlemen concluded with an earnest appeal for the ladies to come forward and help in the work.

WARRINGTON AND THE PARISH COUNCILS BILL.

At the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians held on Wednesday (Mr. G. Ratcliffe in the chair), the CLERK (Mr. J. C. Sutton, C.C.), said he had received a communication from Mr. Hulton, clerk to the Lancashire County Council, calling attention to some of the effects of the Parish Coun- cils Bill,which was practically passed .Ono of these would be the division into wards of all townships with 1,000 or more inhabitants, for the purpose of appointing Parish Councils, and further stating that it would be necessary to do this before the Ist of July next. Some of the town- ships which would require thus dividing were Rixton, Burtonwood and Penketh.He could not say exactly what effect the Bill would have upon the Board of Guardians. one thing bewever,was certain and that was that instead of coming out in April next year the members of the Board will come out in November. Instead of being then returned for the township of Warrington they would be nturned from certain wards, just as the members oi the Town Council were, and the wards would no doubt be the same as the municipal wards, except that there would be one extra for the extra-municipal wards. There would be many importmt matters to decide, however, one of which would be whether Latchford should remain attached to the Rural Authority of Warrington or go to Runcorn, or be a separate authority itself. He thought that the wiser thing to do would be to anpoint a committee to consider the various effects the Bill would have upon them. Ile bad also received a circular from the Local Govern- ment Board of which the following is a copy.— Local Government Board, Whitehall, S.W., March 5. Sir,—l am directed by the Local Government Board to draw attention to the provisions of the Local Govern- ment Act, 1894, which has this day received the Royal Assent, in so far as they affect the election of guardians and members of urban sanitary authorities duriog the present year. Under the Act, urban and rural district councils will take the place of urban and rural sanitary authorities, and parishes in any rural district will be represented on the board of guardians by the persons elected as rural district councillors, guardians as such being only elected in parishes in urban districts. The first elections under the Act of guardians and district councillors will be held on the Bth November next or on such later date or dates in the year 1894 as the board may fix, and the persons elected will come into office on the second Thursday next after their election, or such other day not more than seven days earlier or later as may be fixed by or in pursuance of rules made by the board in relation to their election. Upon the day on which the first guardians and urban or rural district councillors elected under the Act come into office, the rural t sanitary t ar y membersauth oorfi t iboards l of e aguardiansto d or urban n who or ; but until that day the persons who are at the passing of the Act were guardians or members of urban sanitary authorities for districts other than boroughs will continue in office notwithstanding any want of qualification, as i the term of office for which they were elected expire/on that day, and except for the purpose of filling casual vacancies, or of electing additional guardians, where the number is in- creased. no further elections will be held. Hence, the guardians and members of urban sanitary authorities who would have gone out of office in April next but for the passing of the Act, will continue in office until November, and will then retire, and the elections which would otherwise have been held in April will not take place. No further steps therefore, should be taken with a view to any such election.—l am, &c., Huou OWEN, secretary." After a short discussion it was decided to appoint a committee, to consider with the Clerk, the effects of the Bill upon the Warrington Union. •

RUGBY. WARRINGTON V. BARROW.

The first team are indeed enjoying a holiday season The majority of their recent engagements have been played away from home, and under circumstances more or Ines pleasant. In cases where blizzards have thrust themselves in—like last week—the pleasure has of course been "less." On Saturday the team journeyed to Bar- row to meet the premier team of the town in the Lan- cashire Cup Competition. Barrow are certainly an example of what Dame Fortune can do with a club's prospects, no matter what ability the said club may possess. This club of the north seems to have been con- spicuously unfortunate and judging from the displays which they have given in both their fixtures with Warring- ton they appear to be occupying a false position on the statistical table of the competition. Their open,and at all times scientific play, has earned for them the admira- tion of footballers all over the county, who regret for the most part their undignified position so near the bottom of the table. Warrington were without the aid of Broady, James Bates, Buckley, and Jolley, whilst Bar- row had their best team on the field. The teams were of the following composition :—F. Ileesom, back; F. I Barber, C. Potts, P. Carey, and J. T. Massey, three- quarters ; J. W. Evans and John Bate (captain), helf- backs ; Fair. Turner, W. Handles, G. Dakin, J. Taylor, E. Bailey, J. T. Thorniley, J. Berry, and W. Nevins, forwards. Barrow : A. Harley, back; Bell, Dickinson, Carter, and Popplewell, three-quarters; Marshall and Chapman, half tsacks ; Greaves, Gird wood, Bowiter, Sat- terthwaite, Sharpe, Weatherall, I,Voodhouse, and Strongi- tharm, forwards. Barrow pressed Warringten on their lines in the first few minutes, but a sturdy rush of the Warrington forwards soon relieved. The home forwards then sot the ball away in a loose dribble, but they were too eager and over-ran it. Barber then gave his side some fine assistance, after which Turner got possession from a line-cut, and made tracks for the Barrow lines. Dickinson afterwards kicked to .Heesom, and following up, secured his man. Barrow still held the advantage, I Marshall dribbling to the NVarrington back, who saved I well and punted cleverly into touch. The lisme halves were penalised, and Fair Turner had a long shy at goal, which enabled Marshall to make a sprint down the right wing, and putting his men on side the Barrow forwards went away with a rush, Woodhouse being prevented from scoring by the good defensive abilities of Heesom. Evans helped his side out of the difficulty, and passing to Massey, the latter got the ball to half-way. Smart kicking was indulged in by both sets of backs, atter which Chapman made an atterapt to score but was un- successful. The Warrington f )rwards had the -best of the scrummages, and Taylor was only held by Harley just in time on the line. From the pack Evans passed to Potts, who exchanged to 'Massey, the latter getting over the coveted line. Girdwood put in sterling work, and Potts was cautioned by the referee for an unfair tackle of Greaves. Warrington got again into the Barrow 25, a gigantic punt to halfway by Harley sending them back, after which the whistle went for the interval. The game on resuming became rather desultory owing to the fre- quent scrummasses. At length it was relieved by Topple- well, who took the advantage of a kick across the field, ' and, getting hold of the ball, ran splendidly to the War- rington 25. Evans ultimately raised the siege by judi- cious kicks into touch. Getting the ball at their toes, the Barrow forwards bombarded Fleesom, and he was obliged to kick over his own line to save. Wheeling the scrummages, the wire-pullers obtained only temporary relief, as Greaves dribbled nicely and took the tall over the line. He, however, kicked too hard, and Massey sent the ball dead. Warrington then pressed, until Popplewell took the ball finely from Dickinson and ran. into, his opponents' quarters. Thrice in quick succession did he gather the ball splendidly and rushed for the line, but he could not get past the defence. Carey then slsowed his ability in handing off. and with the play at halfway the whistle sounded, with the seers Warring- ton, 1 try ; Barrow, nothing.

KILLED BY THE FALLING OF A WALL.

LAMENTABLE CASE AT RUNCORN.

A sad case of sudden death was on Tuesday morning investigated by Mr. J. E. Worsley,coroner, at the Runcorn Police Court, relative to the demise of Sarah Dunning, a girl, 12 years of age, of Back Brunswick—A., Runcorn. At the inquiry the parents of 'the deceased were represented by Mr. Linaker, and Mr. Burton appeared on behalf of Mr. Edleston,War- rington, the owner of the adjoining property.—The evi- dence showed that the girl at about 2.30 in the afternoon of Saturday, was passing an old dilapidated wall between Wilson's-row and Back Brunswick-st., when it fell upon her. The material covered her all over with the excep- tion of her head, and tie result of the accident was that the girl received shock to the system and notwithstand- ing the efforts of Dr. Robinson, she died the same after- noon. The wall mentioned has been considered for some time to be in a dangerous state, and endeavours had been made to strengthen it by means of prups. These, however, had been removed before the accident oc• curred.—The Coroner, after receiving the evidence, said it seemed pretty dear that the wall belonged to the owners of the property in the court. It was in a ruinous condition, and not lit to be in a neighbourhood whore children wore playing. Evidently, the hole in the wall had been used for a "short cut" by persons living in Back Brunswick-st. It was also evident that the wall had been left in a dangerous and neglectful state for some time. There could not, however, be a doubt that the child was trespassing at the time it met with its in- juries,and consequently the owner of the property could not be held responsible for the accident. Something should be done to have the wall demolished.—The jury recorded a verdict of "Accidental death," and expressed the opinion that the wall was in a disgraceful state, and they asked the Coroner to draw the attention of the Com- missioners to its conslition.—The Coroner replied by say- ing that he was quite willing to do so, and expressed his sympathy with the parents in their bereavement.

LECTURE AT THE RUNCORN LIBERAL

CLUB.

PEACE OR WAR?

On Wednesday evening the third lecture or the winter session was given in the Liberal Club prem- ises, by Mr. J.W.S.Callie, secretary of the Financial Reform Association, on the subject of " The French Revolution and what it cost us." Mr. B. liniveton took the chair, and the attendance was large, amongst those present being Messrs. William Hand- ley, Norman, R. Posnett, S. Coventry, J. Clarke, A. Riley, T. Wood, Boyle, and Captain J. Le-Couteur.— The Chairman in a short and pithy address in trod uced

The lecturer who prefaced his remarks with an apology for his non-appearance a short time ago. Ihe circum- stance was due to a misunderstanding on his own part, he having mis-read the communication which bore the date of his proposed visit. The French revolutionary war was the greatest war in which this country had ever been engaged, inasmuch as it lasted twenty three years and cost us £831,000,000. It was the chief cause of our tremendous national debt which hung, like a heavy mill-stone, round the necks of the people, and which perhaps would never be wiped out. Not only did it cost I us this enormous amount of money, but the loss of life and the misery it entailed were really appalling. France had to bear the brunt of the war more than any other country, and was almost de-populated by it. More than one hundred years ago the French nation were engaged in a similar struggle to that which the workers of to-day were engaged—a struggle between the workers and the idlers. France had, for a long time before the ttevolution been ruled by kings or ministers. Coercion was the order of the day not only on the part of monarchs bat of high ecclesiastics. The poor people had neither liberty of action,speech,nor thought. Further each of the nobles was a small king on his own estate. Such a condition of things could only be supported by the strong arm of the law, aod the prospect of what would happen under a weak king was not reassuring to the privileged classes. Louis XVI was a weak monarch while his wife, Maria Antoinette was a stong willed woman, and it was in their time that the difficulties of the reign of " privilege " came to a head. The lecturer proceeded with a brief survey of the social condition of the people and asserted that at 4the time of the Revolu- tion France was about twice the size of Grsat Britain. It had a population of a little more than twenty five millions. Of those,l6l- millions were agriculturalists,and millions were engaged in trade or commerce. That proportion was sufficient to show that there must have been something radically wrong; for one agriculturist ought to have been able to produce enough food for himself and five or six others. By there being one food producer to five artizans, and by exchanging the result of their labaurs, each one would, sa to speak, be working one-sixth of his time for a bare subsistence and the remaining five-sixths for comforts and luxuries, which would have meant a condition of comfort for each. But in reality things were very d,fferent. Sir A. Allison had said that sup- posing an acre to be worth £3 2s. 7d., the proportion which went to the king was £1 18s. 4d.; to the landlord 19s. and to the actual tenant ss. 3d. He quoted the above historian owing to his being a Whig and therefore prejudiced against the Revolution. The labourer was only- allowed to keep one-twelfth of his produce. The remaining four-twelfths went to the landlord and seven- twelfths to the king. Consequently the poor peasants had to eke out a miserable existence. Comtort, they had none, and Alien a severe winter came, or there was a bad harvest, many of them died of starvation. The minister of religion only appeared to the peasants as a burden like the landlord, for the clergy owned one-third of the land and the tithes amounted to £5,000,000. The nobility and clergy were exempt from most of the taxation. A poor man could not rise to eminence in either military, civil, or religious life. High positions were reserved for those of high birth. When they compared France of 100 years ago to the Great Britain of to-day they found Many points of resemblance. He compared the condition of the Irish tenantry and the Scotch crofters to that of the French peasants. There was, plenty of provision in Scotland for the sustenance of the people, but the crofters dare not touch it. The condition of the people sometimes in large towns was distressing, for they wanted work but could not get it through the action of the owners of the soil. Mr. Callie also pointed out that the taxation in this coun- try was increased from nine millions to thirty-two mil- lions sterling and that the effect upon the National Debt was to raise it from £237,500,000 to £846,000,000. Speaking of the scenes which took plage during the course of the Revolution lie said that men, who had beentremarkable for their extreme moderation and sen- sibility to suffering were transformed into human fiends Were they to consider the question from a British point of view they would see that they would have been far more advanced towards freedom and civilization had it not been that in plunging into the war with France they allowed the aristocracy to fasten their burdens upon them. Dearly the workers of Britain had had to pay for the false step England took, and surely they would take warning, and from the experience of the past,learn wisdom for the future. The were living in a very simi- lar condition of things just now to those of a century since. Once again the workers were demanding a better share of the products of their labour. The privileged classes in every country knew that the most reliable way to crush reform was a big war. The lecturer also referred to the present demand for the increase of the navy and to the panics on the same question in 1884 and 1888, and stated that the present was a good opportunity to advocate the settlement of international disputes by arbitration. They had already had two examples : the Alabama ' claims and the Behring Sea dispute. Let them endea- vour to abolish war and substitute arbitration, so as to assure their revolution being not one of despotism and violence, but that of peaceful evolution from injustice to justice, from darkness to light, and bring about the prophesy uttered more than 1800 years ago, that they should have " Peace on earth and goodwill .towards men." (Loud applause.) aMr. Crosfield, of Liverpool, deprecated the present agitation for the spending of so much money with a view to increasing the navy, and urged the settlement of dis- putes by arbitration so that the people of the world !night live in harmony and peace, one with another. (Cheers.) The Chairman pointed out that in the United King- dom they only grew three months' supply of food dur- ing the year ; thus we were !dependent upon foreign countries to furnish their requirement s for the remain- ing nine months. This necessitated them to have control of the seas. He appreciated the work of the Peace Society, but in his opinion they had not suggested the proper way to settle disputes that arose between nations. What was the use of England's navy falling behind if those of France, Russia, and other powers did not. If ' there was international arbitration it would be one of the grandest things the world had ever seen. Mr. Callie said that thd Society did not ask for impos- sibilities. What they found fault with as members of the Peace Society, was that Government after Govern- ment came in and went out without making any effort to settle disputes by arbitration. The only. cases where this had been done were the Alabama claims, and the Behring Sea Fisheries, and when the historian recorded the history-of the present country, it would be shown' that the greatest deeds dine by Mr. Gladstone would be those in which it had been stated, that he humbled him- self by trying to place right in the place of might. He had laid a noble foundation, and if other Governments would follow up the work, they would find a disappear- ance of the miserable and fearful records of war. (Ap- plause.) On the proposition of Mr. Posnett, seconded by the Chairman, a cordial vote of thanks was passed to the Lecturer, who in acknowledging the compliment pro- posed a similar motion to Mr. Kniveton, for so ably pre- siding. Mr. Lewis seconded, and the proceedings ter- minated, Mr. Kniveton expressing a hope that it would, be again their pleasure to listen to Mr. Cattle during the course of the winter session.

RUNCORN SCIENCE AND ART CLASSES.

ENCOURAGING REPORT.

At the monthly meeting of the Runcorn School Board held in the Town Hall, on Tuesday evening, the Clerk presented the second annual report of the Science and Art Committee which shows that the Science and Art classes have during the present session, been marked by considerable progress,both in number of classes and students attending them. The pupils during the session of 1892-3 numbered 221, but during the session of 18934 this number has been raised to 263. Beneficial changes have been made in the teach- ing staff and in the time alloted to the various subjects. The students also had the full benefit of the excellent collection of apparatus which has been provided for the use of the classes. The attendance was better than in the previous year, and there would have been a larger number sitting for examination but for . the fact that some pupils left the town. The following is a list of the students, showing the certificates gained.—Geometry, Second 1, G. Moss, Charles Edwards. Building Construction, advanced let, Harry Turton ; advanced 2nd, John Speakman, John Hudson ; elementary pass, Harold Davies, William Sillis, Walter Wiswall. Machine Construction, elementary pass, William Bankes, James Seel, George Williamson, Walter Gibbons: advanced 2nd, John A. Bate, John Wilson ; advanced Ist, Reginald Timmins, Edward Har- rington, E. Timmins. Physiography, elementary pass, Mary Hayton, Thomas Coo-lc, Charles Edwards, William Richards, Jessie Ashbrook, Robert Bowyer, Francis Spre.•ton. Chemistry, theoretical, elementary pass,Ellem Crowther, Thomas Jones, William H. Richards, Thomas Buxton; advanced 2nd, John Molyneux. Magnetism and Electricity, elementary pass, Edward Flarringten, John Molyneux, Ellen Crowther, Elizabeth Crosby. Applied Mechanics, elementary pass, Edward Harring- ton. Freehand Drawing, 2nd grade, Walter Howard 2nd class, Harry Crowther 2nd class. Model drawing, John Beck, lit class ; Fred Upton, Ist class. Shading from Cast, elementary stage, William Kerman, 2nd class ; John Beck, 2nd class.— This is the first session to feel the abolition of the 2nd class pass in the elementary stage of science subjects. When it is considered that in the previous session, out of 43 students, 20 gained this certificate, it will be readily understood why the numercial results this ses- sion show a falling off, nut considering the higher standard of attainment, the number of passes gained is on the whole very satisfactory. There is room for im- provement in several subjects, and especially in the results of the art class. The committee hope by special attention to weak points to achieve higher results each year. The amount received from the Runcorn Improve- ment Commissioners was £l7O, of which £46 was spent in apparatus for the classes. The amount of grant earned was £59 183. against £5l 123. in the previous session. Dr. McDougall stated that the Committee were doing their best to encourage the taste for science and art in the town. He hoped their efforts would be attended with great results. They found there were many scholars ready to attend the classes at the earlier parts af the session, but towards the close they dribbled away so that there were scarcely half as many in attendance at the finish as at the opening. This was the difficulty of the committee.—The Chairman expressed the opinion that the Board had done its best to afford technical in- struction as well as art education to the young people of the town and lie thought satisfactory progress had been made.—The report was adopted.

RUNCORN RURAL SANITARY AUTHORITY.

On Tuesday the usual fortnightly meeting of the above Authority was held in the Workhouse,Dutton, under the presidency of Mr. J.Mclntyre. The vice- chair was occupied by Mr.J.Knowles, and the others present were the Revs.J. W.N.Tanner and J.llanby ; Messrs. J. Brandretb,ll. E. Heywood, J.M.Williaros, W. Harrison, E. Aaron, J. Weir, T. Baxter, CC.; C.T. Whitley, W. Pickering. T. Lewis, A. Broom,E.Jack- eon, J.Ash ton (clerk),G. Ashton (deputy clerk),Far- rington (nuisance inspector), and Dr. Adams (medi- cal officer of health).

ALLEGED POLLUTION OF THE SHIP CANAL.

The Clerk read a letter from Mr. Lionel Welts, in re- ference to the complaint of Mr. Whitworth, secretary of the Ship Canal, regarding the filthy state of the water in the canal at Haystack cutting in December last. Mr. Wells stated in that letter that he had called upon Mr. Whitworth to inquire which of the ,many sewers, whose courses, had been severed by the construction of the canal he referred to. It was then pointed out to him that it was Halton Brook. He visited the outfall on the previous day and found the water flowing into the canal was slightly coloured and there was but little sediment in the cesspool through which it flowed. There was nothing objectionable in the liquid which WP.S very much clearer and cleaner than the water in the Ship Canal. He passed another cesspool which was quite full of deposit,and there were no signs of it evec having been cleaned out. It was agreed that the Ship Canal should clean out all the cesspools,and that the Authority should be put to no expense by reason of the works necessary for the construction of the canal.—The Clerk stated that according to the agreement with the Ship Canal they were bound to clear out the cesspools.—The Chairman: There is no ground for complaint against ourselves. The Canal Company are remiss in the performance of their duties. —Mr. Aaron: They will do nothing unless they are forced.—The Rev. J. Hanby suggested that the clause in the agreement be pointed out to the Company.—The Clerk proposed the sending of a copy of the letter and the reference to the agreement to the Canal Company.— This course was ultimately taken, on the motion of the Rev. J. Hanby, seconded by Mr. Aaron.

PROPOSED APPOINTMENT OF A PORT SANITARY AUTIIORITY.

The Authority received a communication from the Lord Mayor of Manchester regarding the Port Sanitary Authority accompanied by the resolution passed at a preliminary- meetnw,which stated that it was desirable to hold a conference of all the local authorities interested in the matter, as riparian owners, at an early date for the purpose of considerinc, the appointment of a joint Board as a Port Sanitary 'Authority.—The Town Clerk of Liverpool wrote enclosing a copy of the report of the Port Medical Officer upon the question as to the consti- tution of a Port Sanitary Authority for Manchester, Sal- ford and Runcorn. He stated that the medical officer had no means of ascertaining whether the Runcorn Sani- tary Authority desired that the duties of their port should be relegated to the Liverpool Port Sanitary Authority. If this should be their desire, and the Local Government Board deemed such an arrangement desir- able, there could be no objection to those duties being discharged by the Authorities of the Port of Liverpool upon the same terms and conditions as apply to the other riparian authorities abutting upon the Liverpool Port. The officer, however, was of the opinion that it wsuld not be desirable that a joint Board for Runcorn and Liverpool should be established.—This matter was:re- ferred to a committee previously appointed.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
I-NVITATION BY TliE LORD MAYOR OF LONDON.

The Lord Mayor of London drew the attention of the Authority, by letter, to the Congress in London of the British Institute of Public Health which was the first op- portunity afforded for local authorities to meet together in the metropolis to discuss questions affecting the per- formance of their duties and the difficulties whin con- stantly arose.—The letter was allowed to lie 'upon the table.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. Adams reported that a case of scarlet fever had occurred in Main-st., Frodsham ; three cases in Overton; and one at "Five Crosses." There %ME also a case of diphtheria in Main-st., Frodsham. There were appar- ently no defective sanitary arrangements, and he had ordered an analysis of the water used in the ,districts. lie had visited the above cases and had issued the us ua sanitary and preventative orders.

NOTICE OF MOTION.

Mr. Williams notified the authority that at the next meeting he would move the re-election of Dr. Adams as Medical Officer of Health, at the salary paid hitherto.

notices upon various owners of property in Weston tP provide a sufficient water supply to prelnise3 owned by them. THE SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RUNCORN.

In his annual report on the Runcorn branch of the Mersey Mission to Seamen, Mr. W. Shaw, the local missionary, states that the work of themisEion has been very successful amongst the sailors and watermen. Since the Runcorn docks have been reopened the work of the mission has gone on much as usual. The small mission room at Weston Point has been given up since the opening of the docks ; it has been of immense value to the work, and Nve trust many seamen have been greatly helped by the services held there. We have had some sad acci- dents during the past year, resulting in the deaths of several seamen. We have done all we could to assist relatives and friends, and in man), cases the lunerals started from our church many captains and seamen attending. The balance of debt which remai-ied on the Insti- tute at the beginning of the year ha 3 been wiped off owing to the successful bazaar and donations of friends of the mission. The Runcorn Building Gatrimittae neid their last meeting on the 9th of June, when a vote Of thanks was proposed and carried to the many. friends who had assisted them to build the institute, which has proved such a boon t-s seamen and boat-people visiting the port. We have services on Sunday morn:ng and evening, The holy communion is administered on tbe second Sunday in the month, Canon Maitland Wood or one of his curates taking the service. We are assisted by a staff of volunteer workers,many masters of vss3els helping when in port. A Sunday school for canal boatmen's children is carried on regularly with the help of 14 teachers. There are regular wsek-day services, concerts, magi.° lantern lectures, ace. ; the seamen are visited on their vessels, flats, or beats, and also at their houses in three; of sickness ; 240 letters Lave been written by sailors, and 74 bags of books given away. On the 14th of March ?2 candidates belonging to the Mission were confirmed jo the Parish Church. The different entertainments at Christmas and Whitsuntide were most successful, and we had much blessing during the week of special ser vices. During severe frost at the beginning of the year the canal boat-people suffered greatly, and with the a 4: sistance of some friends we were enabled to give 76 dinners to canal boat children and their parents, for which they were extremely grateful. The amount ob- tained in subscriptions for the branch amounted to £55 145. 21., and the receipts towards the building fulla in the same period were £133 124. 10d , the Pr°- needs of the bazaar being that sum with the exception of 63. 4d.

CHARGE OF PERMITTING Dlt UNE. ENNESS.--' INTERESTING CASE. At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, before Messrs. A.
