WIDNES WoMEN'A LIBERAL ASSOCIA-

TION.

THE RETIREMENT OF MR. GLADSTONE. On Monday night the annual tea and meeting in connection. with the Widnes Division Women's Liberal Association was held in tke Drill Hall,Wid- nes. There was a large attendance. The tables were presided over by the following ladies : Mesdames Brady, Devlin, Flynn, Bridgestock, Sadler, Owens, O'Donnell, Walsh, liodgkinson, McElroy, Ireland, Roberts, Leather, Wharton, Shores, Misses Deacon, tledley, Canavan, Cowley, Young, Foster, Dwyer, Kyle, Morrison, Calvert, Colquitt, Wylie, Johnson, Caldwell, O'Keefe, Wilkinson, Maddison. and Kenrick. The after pro- ceedings were presided over by Mrs. E. Stewart Brown (president oF. the association). The following letter was read from Mr. H. Wade Deacon :—"I am very sorry not to be present this evening at the meeting of the Women's Liberal Association, but, following my doctor's advice I am giving up all evening meetings for a time. Our minds are full of sympathy with Mr. 'Gladstone in the great trouble that has come to him ; and in his retirement from the high office he filled so well he carries with him our warmest wishes for his future welfare. His resigna- tion as soon as he felt physically unfitted to fill his post, is simply the crowning act of the splendid loyalty he has shown to the party he has led so long. To the last he has pointed out the way, and we believe that in Lord Rosebery we have a leader who will follow the path of progress, and guide us on to further victories. I sin- cerely hope that the meeting of the Women's Associa- tion may be as successful as you can desire." Mr. Stuart Deacon moved the re-election of Mrs. E. Stewart rirown as president,also the vice-presidents and other officers, and said he could not help referring to the present political state of affairs. Mr. Gladstone, after so many years' service was compelled to retire, and his place would now be filled by Lord Rosebery. This ap- pointment had been challenged in certain quarters, but he (the speaker) theught it would have been more wise if the challenge had been made more privately. Many were of the opinion that Lord Rosebery would drop the Home Itule Bill. If so,he (the speaker) thought he would lose the vets of every decent Liberal and Radical in the country. (Loud applause.) The motion re-electing the officers was seconded by Miss Handley and carried. Miss Carrie Deacon, hon. secretary, read the annual report, which showed the steady progress the associa- tion was making. The number of members now was 760, and a branch association had been formed at Gars- ton. Mrs. O'Keefe, hon. treasurer, submitted the balance sheet for the year. The year was commenced with a balance in hand of £26 9s. 11d.,and closed with a credit balance of £34 15s. 3d.—The reports were adopted, on the motion of Mrs. Williams and Mrs. Fairclough. Mrs. Stewart Brown returned thanks for re-electing her as president, and in referring to the political crisis said it was with very deep regret they heard of the re- tirement of their beloved and venerable leader. He had retired with a glorious record of work done,and his last speech in the House of Commons made the Parish Coun- cils Bill become law. (Applause.) They could not,how- ever, but regret that Mr. Gladstone had not the satis- faction of accomplishing the task he had set himself to do in regard to Ireland ; but they knew on whom to lay the blame. (Hear, hear.) It was not Mr. Gladstone. The House of Lords had come between him and Ireland. (Hear,hear.) But they believed the Home Rule question was only deferred, and would yet become law. The speaker then made a vigorous appeal to the women of Widnes to take an interest in the association, and thus help on the cause of Liberalism.. Mrs. O'Keefe moved a resolution condemning the House of Lords in opposing measures of reform passed by representatives or the people in the HOUSe Of COT.nt mons. seoonneel thd esohiticn, and said she [hough t the House of Lords required mending, not end- ing.---The motion was carried unanimously. This concluded the kituines.s Qf the meeting. A concert, which was very successiiii, ti.. 3 next proceeded with. THE PRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE COMPANY.

Among the various assurance societies, which have sprung into existence during the past half century—and whose benefits to the people are so inestimable—there are nonewhich stand out in such colossal proportions as the Prudential Company. Since its formation forty-five years ago,the company has accumulated a large amount of public confidence—and accordingly support—which, we venture to think, no other company at present in exigence can hope to vie with, at least for many years to come. Nor can this success be attributed—as can the prosperity of some companies—to what is described in fanciful language, as " a favoured turn of fortune's wheel." Therrudential Assurance Coinpany, undoubtedly owes its present im- pregnable position to the soundness of the basis upon which it was built, and to the fairness and unfaltering integrity which is ever present in its transactions. That is how the company has achieved the possession of the universal confidence which now characterises it. This year—as may be seen from an advertisement on our front page—the agents of the company have been mar- vellously successful, in the very face of obstacles of desperate potency, and disappointments of a depressing and disheartening character. From the commencement of the year to its close—over Warrington as elsewhere— there passed a wave of trade depression, and epidemic, which impoverished and pauperised the people by the thousands. Especially in districts like Warrington— which were affected by the great coal strike—were the thrifty habits of the poor wrecked, at least temporarily, and the people often confronted with nothing less for- miclable than starvation. Yet during all this, the com- pany have been able to increase their assets by £2,229,213 making a total of £18,538,865, and to distribute over a million and a half sterling among the poor ! In War- rington and district alone the Prudential Compaty, during that disastrous strike, very generously justified the confidence phced in it by the working classes, by assisting these in need almost as extensively as an insti- tution organized for the purpose could have done. It is well known that for years past the company has issued " free" policies—that is, free from any further payment —to those who, after paying for five years, find them- selves either unable or unwilling to 'keep up the pay- ments. There are now 338,272 such policies in force, of which no less 55,76 t were added during the oast year, the term of five years being in many cases reduced, out of charity ; whilst there were also many hundreds of policy holders assisted by the company to keep up their policies. Out of the total number of " free ' policies no less than 6,424 became cliims during the year. Altogether, out of the 314 millions of people in Great Britain, there are now 10,476,393 assured with the com- pany ; the average duration of the policies—which is very satisfactory—being seven years and a quarter. The number of assured persons who died during the year was 185,003; 102 of that number foundered with the " Victoria " and 72 were victims of the colliery explosion at Thornhill. Among the claims which arose during the year it might be mentioned that one case was that of the late well-known piper`to H.B.H. the Prince of Wales, another was the English fireman who was killed at Chicago Exhibition, and another the unfortunate man who was shot in the Featherstone Riot. In the district of Warrington there is no doubt that last year,the Com- pany gave a great impetus to its own prosperity, by the generous, yet unostentatious manner, in which it unhesitatingly stretched forth the hand of succour to the vast army of needy ones in their hour of distress. At that time the district was under the superintendence of Mr. Lythcroe ; he has since removed to another district, and has been succeeded by Mr. N. Gorton, under whose superintendence there is no doubt the Society will con- tinue to prosper in this neighbourhood. The item in the financial statement of the Company, which is particu- larly worthy ot pointing out, is the increase of the assets for the year by £2,229,213. Everyone must agree that men wha can add two and a quarter millions sterling to the funds of an institution, dealing with the working classes, in such a year as 1893, are worthy of the unstinted confidence of their constituents.

A NEW GAME.—A new game of skill called" Parlour Football " has been invented by Mr. W. B. Lawson, 18, Foyle Terrace, Dublin. Lovers of the game can there- fore play it either in the parlour or on the field. It is modelled much on the lines of Association Football, and is intended for two players. The field is represented by a board which contains seventy-seven squares, three at each end being marked off as goals. The rival teams are represented by pieces, or men, each team being coloured and shaped differently for easier identification. Thelobject of each Player is so to manceuvre the men that they push the ball through his opponent's men, and over either of the three squares which form his goal. The price of the game is is., or is. 3d. post free from the inventor. CONDITION OF THE COAL TRADE.

COLLIERIES ON SHORT-TIME.

THE QUESTION OF SURPLUS LABOUR. In a conversation with a newspaper representa- tive on Wednesday, Mr. T. Glover, miners' agent, said the coal trade of St. Helens appeared to be very dull, and the men were working on an average about feur days a week. He attributed this to the idea which prevailed at the conclusion of the strike that there would be another stoppage in February, and as a consequence increased orders were given, necessitating about six days' work for the men. At some of the collieries a little stacking of coal was going on, and in all probability manufacturers had done some stacking in their yards.- This would find its level, and he did not think' the outlook for the summer was bad. When asked wlaether there were not too many men at work in the pits, Mr. Glover replied that there was no question abou*, that, and it was a very difficult matter to' deal with. When the miners' wages began to advance —and they had advanced about 40 per cent. in five years —there was a rush of unskilled labour to the mines. Men who had had ni experience in coal-getting were taken on, and put to work with skilled men, the only apparent object being that these men would work for less money, and wages would be brought down. There were now about 600,000 miners at work in the country, or about 100,000 more than there ought to be. Anyone could imagine what the effect of such a state of things had been. There might be acme solution to the difficulty in a proposal to insert a clause in the Coal Mines Amend- ment Act to the effect that no man should be allewed to work in a coal mine unless he had commenced work about a mine before he was eighteen years of age. Min- ing was called unskilled labour, but really it ought to be classed as skilled labour. At the present time it did not matter what ago a man was or whether he had been in a mine before, and a clause in the present Act that a man should not get coal unless he had been at work about a mine for two years was almost a dead letter. Mr. Glover does not think there will be any at- tempt to reduce wages this year, as the employers who are members ot the Coalowners' Federation were evi- dently trying to keep prices up. It was, however, Un- fortunate that some masters outside the federation were underselling them. Mr. Woods, M.P., also expresses the decided opinion that far too many men are at work in the mines of the country.

THE Sr. HELENS MURDER TRIAL.—The trial of John Carney, John Leahey, and Frank Riley, for the murder of Police-constable Gordon, at St. Helens on the 13th November, is fixed for Tuesday next, at St. George's Hall, Liverpool. It is stated in St. Helens that a fund is being raised for the defence of the men.

MAGAZINE STATISTICS.—" From "The Newspaper Press Directory" for 1894 we find that there are now pub- lished in the United Kingdom 2,061 Magazines, of which more than 471 are of a decidedly religious character. Comparing 1894 with 1860 (the first year that any com- plete list of magazines was published) we find that in that year there were only 405 of such publications in existence, 162 of which were religious magazines. Al- most every branch of science and thought is represented, and it is curious to note that already the latest cult, Theosophy, has no less than five publicatious devoted to its advocacy."'

DESTITUTION IN WASRENCITON.

Sir,—l have been a close observer of events in War- rington for the last 20 years or more. During the coal strike,l took an active part to try to relieve those wham I thought were worthy of it. So far as that strike is concerned, its seriou% resulcs have passed away; hut there is one calamity almost approaching to it as tar as destitution is concerned. I now allude to ne stoppage of the Howley factory,thus throwing out of employment 150 females at least, without any prospect of starting. I very seriouAly ask what is going to be aon, with them ? I understand there was a surplui of -.2150 or more left from the fund collected on behalf of .the coal strike. Cannot this surplus fund be now available for the relief of this large number of people, many of whom I know are starving ? No better use, I feel sure could be made of it.—Yours truly, WILLIAM DIGGLE.

Warrington, March 6th, 1134. "THE POLICE COUI:1 TESTAMENT."

Sir,—From time to time I am called upon to give evidence in our Police Coo- t, and it has often stru3k, me on being sworn that the et py of the Ti-stament offered for your osculation is certainly not as clean as it might be. On Monday last I was a witness- in a case,and had to go through the painful and dangerous ordeal of kis- sing this dirty copy. Why not cover the book with a white ealie,-) cover.vvhiCh could he washed and changed, say every week? EATER VES!RIES.

Sir,—As the season is approaching it behoves all lovers of Gospel truth to attend these meetings which by law all payers of the poor rate are entitled to do so, irres- pective of what Archbishop Benson or Bishop Thorold may think or write. The Church of England is a State establishment,hence every man and woman has a voice in its affairs. Men professing, tm he Protestants and Christians ought to protest against hymns ancient and modern,.Parish Magazine, alibi "Dawn of Dly." Enter your protest against early -celebrations ; they are con- trary to the Scriptures, and the prayer of consecration in the communion offin,-. These Italian imitators now tell us : Only come to 8 a.m. low mass fasting and the rest Of the Sabbath day you can enjoy yourselves,boat- ing,cricketing;or even reading the "Sporth< Chronicle." Enter your protest against the clergy or choir turning their hacks upon you dui ing .ny part of the service. Demand the restoration of evening communion. Never stand during procession of parsons and singers. Who are they? In many cases your own paid servants. It rests with the laity to end or mend the establishmert. A vicar wrote me last month : " I quite agree with you that there is a growing feeling for diseqtablishment. The sooner the better,the bishops think more of the mainten- ance of the corporation of the Church. of England than for the maintenance of the principles of the Reforma- tion."—Yours truly, Frodshana, Match 6, 1894.

THE RIVER AND THE SHIP CANAL.

Sir,—The litigation, reported in the " Examiner," be- tween Messrs. Fairclough and the Canal Company ought, I think, to open the eyes of those interested in the well-being of the borough, as to the danger that exists of the detrimental action of the Canal upon the naviga- tion of the river. I think it must be plain to every thinking person that if, even at this by no means the driest season of the year, the Canal is taking more than its legal allowance of our river water, it will want more than that when the dry season is upon us. A recur- rence of a season like our last dry summer would result in the silting up of the tidal channel above Runcorn. As a silting agent the tide is our enemy, only to be effi- ciently counteracted by the down-scour of natural drain- ago waters. Every gallon of this taken from us is a loss. How will it be when the thrle cOMO—as ColM3 it must sooner or later—when there is not water encugh to supply tN needs of both waterways? Will it be the big city and the canal, or the comparatively little town anti The river, that will have to give way and suffer the loss of water traffic? The big ba.tallion iS the nrin Most favoured by Fate. Messrs. Fairclough in this have been fighting the battle of the town as well as their own, and ought to have whatever of help trid sympathy can be given in this good cause, While it would be a sin—nay worsts a blunder to unduly hamper the Ship Canal Co., the rights and interests of our town must he zealously safe-guarded. The loss of the river as a commercial water-way would be a sad blow to Warrington. Let the siversCommitteelook to ft.—Truly yours.

JOHN PEERS.

THE ToWN CLOCK.

Sir,—l should like to assure" F.W.R." that it was in ro spirit of captiousness that I ventured to remark upon his note on above subject. I believe WP are animated by the same desire: to see our town endowed with at least one good public clock. At present the public clocks of Warrington are scarcely worth more than the dignity of being kitchen monitors—something, by which to boil potatoes. There is not a public clock in the town—or a private one either lam afraid—by which Greenwich mean time can be got to within ono seecnd, that is, not to depend upon. Accidents will happen sometimes —for even a standing clock must be right twice every 21. hours.

Your correspondent will not be offended with me if 1 say that, to me, his methods are not such as to promise a very near approximation to the truth. Firstly, he has not a watch that can he trusted to carry he time from the Landing stage to Warrington within 0-2.5 sec., nor,had he got true time so near as that,could he tell the stroke of the hour to so fine a degree. Twenty five hundredths of a second are not to he computed by such rough and ready methods. F.W.R. sneaks of hav- ing no difficulty in noting the time of striking to a quarter of a second (0-25). As this is a feat of horn. logical accuracy of which I am as yet incapable• would he kindly ear more fully how it is done? Again may I suggest that the striking of a clock on ewes so wide apart as 7th, 14'h, 19th, 28th, &c., is no test of accuracy at all. The clock might be right on all these ears, and yet have a wretchedly had rate. It is the clock which has the least daily variation that would be nronminced the best i.e. to have the hest daily rate. In F.W.R.'s table we have no indication of this; nor do we know how often the clock has been altered in the intervals. As to Russell's window clock, that is not horotolicallY perfect. It stands upon its own bottom, which is what no clock aspiring to the dignity of regelator should do. Tubs ought to do this, but not clocks of any pretentions to accuracy. . . _. . - - --

May I suggest the Leigh time signal at 10 p.m. as bettor for F.W.R. than the Birkenhead gun. The signal can be seen from elevated parts of Warrington on clear nights in the N. E. Some 15 or 16 VPq rq non I Fa,' often the pleasure of seeing it from the L.& NW. Railway bridize, Dallam-lane. If the locality is 'la too much built up it may be seen from there now. But after all nature herself provides us with the best regulator any dear night, in the stars, the ultimate appeal for herological aecuranr. And just now we have, perhaps. the best of these in the dog-star (a Canis Majo- ris) southing at 'Warrington this evening (Saturday. March 10th) at about 7h. 37 min. 2 see, and will do so 3 to. 55.915. earlier every night. So that if this star be watched to disappear behind a straight edge through a

pinhole—both being fixed—it will beat even Russell's as a regulator. Sincerely wishing F.W.R. may continue in the go3d work he has begun, I remain, yours truly, HICKOhY DICKORY.

THE SEED PROBLENI: A CORRECTION, A SUGGESTION, AND A CHALLENGE.

Sir,—ln my note of Saturday last, by a slight error, the conditions I attached to the offer I made to " Ben Chayil" were not clearly stated. My proposal is this : " That for the present the subject be adjourned, say until November next. Then, if " Ben Chayil" his own name, re-open the question, I will engale v.) furnish him, and all who think with him in this matter, with such proof of the fallacy of his ci,ntention as shall satisfy the most rigid investigation. That is my correc- tion. The suggestion is—that " Ben Chayil" and Mr. Howell say no more upon this subject at present ; because the more they do say the more of their own words will they have to eat in the autumn, and " least said soonest mended." But our Catholic friend need not wait so long to eat some of his own " leek." On Saturday he denies what he said a fortnight before. I have both letters before me. In the first he says :—" What do they mean by die ?" The Christian means a " dissolution of soul and body." . . . . Ihe grain of wheat falls into the ground and dies, in the Christian meaning of the term." Now if I may presume to have any understanding of plain English, this certainly means that a grain of wheat ! has a soul ! Positively it must be so if it " dies " in the "Christian meaning of the term." A great deal may I be forgiven poor Mr. Howell as a bemused theologian. He is so accustomed to the use of meaningless phrases that I really believe he does not know, one half of his time, the true tenor of his writings. For instance, now he says—" I did not say nor infer that a seed had a " conkions life." Compare that statement with his previous one. " Mr. Peers being a materialist means the absolute extinction of conscious lire." Now as no "life " but vegetable life is under consideration—veget- ables must, according to the Howell theory, have con- ! scious life. If this be not the meaning, then it has no meaning for me. And now as to the challenge. If " Ben Chayil " has sufficient manliness and gentlemanly feeling to ask, at the end of the time specified, for an explanation and confutation, it shall be accorded to him. But if he has not the courage of his opinions, his opinions are not r much worth,—Your:!;, Ecc.,

JOHN rEERs.

TOWN COUNCIL NOTES.

Alderman Francomb again presided at the meet- ingof the WarringtonTownCiuncil on Tuesdayeven- ing, and opened the proceedings with the intima- tion that the Mayor is now improved in health. Alderman Sutton thereupon took the occasion to auggest that a congratulatory reolut ion toe sent to his Worship, and Alderman Holmes having promptly signified his support, the suggestion as agreed to unanimously. A little routine business followed. It wasreported that two elective audi- tors had been elected, and that Councille.r A. Ben- nett is to be the Mayor's auditor for :he coming year. The return of Mr. Baird for Orford Ward was reported, and there having been lately the death of three councillors or ex-councillors votes of condolence were passed withthe families of the late Councillor Bonnell, the late Mr. R. W. Marsh, and . the late Mr. N. B. Percival.

The minutes of the Finance Committee do not ' usually contain anything of a contentious charac- ter, but on Tuesday evening they afforded the material for a lively debate. Conn. Roberts chal- lenged the passage of the accounts on the ground that they included a sum of £5 odd which had been expended in an irregular fashion. It was the amount which had been paid for the supper given to the firemen, and partaken of also by some mem- bers ot the Fire Engine Committee and others,when the new fire engine was first trieA a few weeks ago. Cluncillor Roberts pointed out that this was a new departure in regard to the expending of public money, which might lead to regrettable results. Heretofore when a Committee had felt generous either towards themselves or others the custom had been for the members to put their hands in their own pockets and generously defray the expenses. But the present fit of -genero- sity was a "sham fit" and the Corporation had been called upan to pay for it. Cauncillor Roberts therefore objected on principal to '.he account and moved that it be not paid, and Councillor James Fairclough seconded the proposal, The defence of the Committee was undertaken by Councillor Hut- chinson, the chairman. It was not often, be said, that they got a new tine engine at Wartington— this waa only the second since Warrington was a borough—and it was really thought that "something should be done to celebrate the occasion." It Wit, a very modest supper, too, including such humble embellishments as beer and lemonade and soda, and pipes and tobacco. Alderman Sutton interposed as a peace-maker. He was of opinion that they should admit extenuating circumstances this time but tell the committee not to do it again.

In the debate which followed the importance of the principle raised was admitted, hut, several councillors thought tilt principle should carried further and not applied to a firemen's supper only. There were the trips of the School Attendance Com- mittee to the ship Clio at. Bangor, said Councillor Muir. What about them? Then, added Councillor Cannel!, in his hot impetuous way : What about all the cost, of the deputations to Longford depot, and what about the cost of the "feed" on the Helvetia when the Corporation went, up the Ship Canal? Councillor Shaw thought. that, if they were going on principle, he might even cavil at the cost of the cabs which conveyed the members of the Corpora- tion to a funeral at the Cemetery lately. A princi- ple applied all round, be argued. Councillor Monks agreed with Mr. Roberts as to the inadvisability of the expenditure, but did Mot think the Committee should be now compelled to pay it. out of thslirown pockets which would be the case if the amendment were carried. The action of tte Committee was suprmted by Councillor Smethurst and condemned by Councillor Plimpton who had been under the impression that the cost of the supper had been privately defraye:. A vote was then taken and the proposal of Councillor Roberts was defeated by 18 to six votes. Councillor Heaton, however, declared woglq pay thc account and that it. should not come out of the rates.

More rapid progress was now made with the business of passing the minutes. It was inciden- tally mentioned by Councillor Monks and Council- lor Beaman that the well-sinking at Longford depot had now turned out successfully, and that there would be an ample supply of water for many years to come. In connection with the School Atten- dance Committee, Councillor Shaw Green alluded to the necessity for truant school accommodation, for the intractable youth of Warrington. Another matter in connection with this committee gave rise to a rather animated discussion. The Committee had resolved that Councillor Shaw should be its deputy-chairman and this resolution appeared on its minutes, but it now seemed that the Commit- tee, after mature reflection, had repented of this rash act, and Councillor Green moved the minutes without this particular resolution. Some curiosity was at once evinced as to the reasons for this " dropping "Mr. Shaw.ancl Alderman llarrison went so far as to declare in legal phrase that it would be ultra vires for the Council to interfere with any , Committee in its appointment of chairman or de- puty-chairman. This led to an appeal to the Town Clerk to define the relative powers of the Council and its Committees. It then appeared that the Council had really very little direct authority over at least three of its Committees, namely, the School -Attendance Committee, the Watch Committee and the Museum Committee. These satellites could do pretty much as they liked; they could pass an se- solution they had a mind to pass, and they cutild stick to those resolutions in spite of the Council. But, on the other hand, the Council has full control of the finances; it can. if the Committees get re- calcitrant,cut them off with (or without) a shilling. and as it is the money that makes the Committees to go, these minor bodies are not so independent after all. In this particular instance, however,the Coun- cil decided to leave the School Attendance Com- mittee to deal with its deputy-chairman as it thought fit.

The rest of the business was not of an impor- tant character. A protest was made by Councillor Hutchinson against a proposal to charge a penny instead of a half-penny for" bespeaking "books at the library, but the protest was not effectual. The rapid growth of the expenditure in connection with the removal of the Park wall was commented upon but as the Committee has not yet exceeded the sum voted to it, the matter was not pressed, and the remaining items in the evening's pre•gramme were " executed " With commendable despatch.

Messrs. Burns' S.F. Grampus, which left Glasgow on • , Tuesday afternoon at four o'clock, got alongside the berth at Pomona Doeks on Wednesday afternoon at 3.50, and the cargo was all delivered in the city by 10 a.m Thursday. This is solar:, work, and shows what expe- dition can be giver 4 via the Ship Canal.

MEETING AT TIIE LIBERAL CLUB.

A met-:ung of the Warrington Women's Liberal A.sociat ion was held in the Liberal Club, on Toeci- day night, when the chair was occupied by Mrs.Z. Armitage. There was a good attendance of the lady members, and Councillors Roberts and PlumPt.on, and Mes,•rz. W. Pierpoint, C. J. .1-lolmes, J. H. nem- ing,Harrison, and Holford, were also preent. She had received letters from Mrs. Crostield and Mks B. Dutton, regretting their inability to be present at the meeting that night. They both hoped that it might be a successful invetintr._ _

Miss SOUTHALL, of Birmingham, next addressed the meeting. She expressed her pleasure at being present at the meeting. She was glad that they were going to have regular meetings. She thought it was quite time that women realised that they ought to be just as much interested in politics, and just as awakened to a sense of their own responsibilities as the men. There was plenty of work to be done. Tee time had gone by when it was necessary to apologise for the political woman, and they should realise that a woman did not become unwomanly by taking part in political work, as could be shown by the examples of the Countess of Aberdeen and Lady I Henry Somerset, who had done se much for the cause of Liberalism and temperance. (Applause.) They some- I times heard about wernan's sphere as though it were en- , closed by four walls, and that her time was to be taken up in darning stockings or mending clothes. (Laughter.) They would be the first to condemn the neglect of home dinks, but they must also re dise that there were wider duties to undertake. It wee the mothers who had the greae responsibility of bringing up the children who would 'be the men and women of the next generation. It was rather too late to define the meaning of Liberal and Con- strvative, but they eheuld always remember that the {Liberals belooofl te the party of progress and reform, that they wanted to go ahead and get something (lents, while the Conservatives liked to stay where they were and keep things exactly as they had been. The Liberals believed in government of the people by the people for the people, and they believed in extended franchise, whereas the Tories believed in government by the few. Miss Southall then went on to I describe the formation of the Women's Liberal I Association which she said now had a membership of I 100,000. The association had done a great deal of hard work for the Liberal party. It was intended to counter- act the Primrose League. She hoped, however, that they would never imitate the Prinirosie League. They had an ruling dailies or ruling councillors, but had only the card of membership, and they believed in educa- tional work. She had heard that the Primrose League enrolled babies. The Liberals dirt not disapprove of young children being trained into Liberalism, but they preferred to have as members of their associa,tion,persons who had come to years of discretion. The association was ti help the Liberal party in parliamentary elections and also in the municipal elections, and they would no doubt have something to do .in the diatriet and parish council elections which would shortly take place. She could not impress too much upon women the importance of electing the beet persona as it was as important to them as to the men, and in some respects more so. It was of equal importance to have good water and good sanitary conditions as to the men. Besides the work at elections there was much educational work to he done, and this was an important duty of the associa- tion. She hoped that the women present would study . the industrial question the question of womeu's suffrage, and this temperance question. The Liberal party had I been taunted with having so much in the Newcastle programme. It was said that there was ton much to tie done. Well, she thought it was a good dell better to have a programme, than to have no programme at all, and to do nettling. (Applause.) The Uniouists were very ready to taunt them, but they had no programme unless it was a programme of negation, if that were possible. (Hear, hear.) In Birmingham they were unfortunately represented ty six Unionists and one Conservative, although they were very much the same. The Unionists were more Tory than the Tories themeelves. They- grumbled at the time that the Gov- ernment had spent in their legislation, but whose fault was it ? It was absurd to say that it was the fault of the Government. The Government had pissed their gieat measures through the House of Commons, and one had passed into law, one of the others had bren spoiled by the House of Lords, and had to be abandoned, and the other had been contemptuously rejected by them. They comptained that the Home Rule Bib had not been adequately discussed, but 84 days had been spent upon it in the House of Commons while the House of Lords had rejected it in four days. They must not think that the Home tile question lied been put aside, however, and they must not forget the loyal sepport which the Irish members gave to labour questions and to the lab- our party in the House of Commons. SERIOUS FIRE AT WARRINGTON.

TWO HORSES. BURNED TO DEATH„

At 220 a.m. yesterday morning a call was received at the fire station, that t fire had broken out at the stables of Mr. Jonathan Broadhurst, greengrocer, opposite the Horse and Jockey Inn, Winwick-road. The brigade under Superintendent Turner with the steam fire engine " Major," in charge of Engineer Ratcliffe, at once proceeded to the conflagration and on their arrival found the stables all in flames two horses having been burnt to death. They at once got to work with a power- Lii from the main there being a good supply of water, and the engine was not put into operation. The fire was got under in half-an-hour. The stables were completely gutted. The cause of the fire is unknown. Fortunately the property was insured. Oae of the horses burned belonged to Mr. Jackson, coal dealer, and the other to Mr. Broadhurst.

AN OUIRAGEOUS AND COWARDLY HUSBAND.

AS F XEMPLARY SENTENCE.

At the Warringtom Borough Court on Thursday (Alderman Sutton in the chair),John Lee, Academy- street, was charged with having assaulte4 his wife, Ellen, on the 21st of February and the 6th March. Defendent was further charged with having ntlec- ted, illtreated, and exposed his three children, Olive •Ethel, aged eight years, Harold, aged three, and Vineent, aged one.—Ellen Lee defendant's wife stated that on Sunday last she went to Urmston at the request of her husband to visit a relative. She returned home on the Monday afternoon, and found her husband drunk. He asked her if she had brought any money or &Wiling back, and on her replying in - the negative he took hold of a fire shovel and struck her across the shoulders. Thu following day he was again drunk, and the police had to come to the house. Other evidence W3S given in support of this charge. On the second charge Mr. Buchton, who appeared on behalf of the R.S.P.C.C., said that the husband when in drink gave his wife no money, and she was obliged to take in lodgers. The husband could earn £2 a week. As to the charge of exposure, defendant frequently turned the wife into the street at night, and the children being afraid followed her. When the inspector visited the house he found the children poorly clad, and with- out fned..--Inspector Uphill stated that on Wednesday merning he visited the defendant's house irfAcademy-st. was accompanied- by Detective Monaghan. The three chilcfren were poorly nourished and badly clothed. There was no food in the house. He went across the road and bought some buns, which the children ate greedily. The mother told him that the children had had nothing to eat that morning. On the 23rd of January witness visited the house, and found the defendant the worse for drink. Witness then severely warned Min.—After further evidence, the Chairman said that the Bench were of opinion that the conduct of the defendant had been outrageous and cywardly. They believed the wife to be a good and industrious waman. The defendant would have to go to prison for three months with hard labour. A separation order would be granted to defendant's wife, the defendant to pay 12s. a week towards his wife's support. On the second charge the magistrates would record a conviction.

GYMNASTIC DISPLAY AT THE

GYMNASIUM.

A most successful assault-at-arms and gymnastic display was given in the Gymnasium, Palmyra Square, Warrington, on Thursday evening, and that the Warrington public are interested in the per- formances of members of the gymnas.ium was evi- dent by the large attendance. The entertainment was mole considerably more attractive by the ap- pearance or Sergt. Major Haigh, Lieut. McClure,and Messrs. J. Davies and Holmes, of Manchester, who gave several performances during the evening. Mr. Thos. :.:lorris, F.G.S.. who has been one of the most ardent supporters of the " Gym." since its com- mencement in the town, presided. The programme opened with a dumb-bell exercise by 13 of the elder members conducted by Mr. J. Kinsley, The exercise was performed with precision and skill, and the audi- dence showed their approval by loud applause. A con- test (sword v. bayonet) was next given by Lieut. McClure and Mr. Holmes. The contestants presented a novel appearance with their screened faces and guarded breasts. and the exhibition caused much interest. Sere. Major Haigh gave his decision in favour of Lieut. McClure who used the sword. Mr. J. E. Warren and Sergt. Major Haigh followed with a fencing contest, and contrary to expectation,Mr. Warren succeeded in scoring two points to the Sergt. Major's one, and the result was received with enthusiastic applause by the audience. A very clever performance on the rings was next given by half-a-dozen members led by Mr. Kinsley. An Italian duel with sabres by Mr. Holmes and Lieut. McClure, re- sulted in a victory for Mr. Holmes. The parallel bar exorcise proved to be an interesting item, and several members showed great efficiency in this department of physical exercise. A number of metnhers next filed into the room with their Indian clubs, and showed their muscular powers to advantage in a performance with them. A boxing match between Lieutenant McClure and J. Davies was watched with close attention. The first two rounds were evenly contested, but in the third, Davies proved too good for his compeer, who gave up the contest. The ' most interestine feature of the evening was the sword feats by Sergeant-Major Haigh, who is considered the best swordsman in the British army. His feats com- prised, cutting an apple on a man's neck, and also on the hand,eevering a broom haudle whilst placed on two glasses filled with water ; cutting a sheep in two at one stroke; and what is known as the razor feat, that of severing a broom handle resting on hoops of paper which are placed on edges of two razors. The feat was performed without the razors cutting the paper. All the feats were executed without the slightest hitch, and the audience frequently broke out into applause. In the vaulting and jumpine exercises,J.Podmore was far and away the best man. He vaulted as high as six feet six inches, and in jumping managed to clear five feet three inchei. On the horizontel bar Mr. Einsley again showed his skill and received the plaudits oT the audience. Several excellent exhibitions were also given by members of the advanced classes. Lieutenant McClure and Mr. Holmes engaged inecombat with rapiers and daggers, using a cloak for protection. Sword and lance exercises were also given under the leadership of Sergeant Haigh. The display concluded with the vaulting horse exercise in which liamber was conspicuous with one or two heavy falls. Miss Stirrup was the pianist of the evening. The members of the Gymnasium whe took part in the exercises during the evening were Messrs. J. Podrnore, J. E. Warren. G. Vernon, A. Vernon, F. C. Bennion, A. Dickens, W. Fletcher. G. Clay, J. Vernon, C. Noble. W. Bember, A. (sigh. A. Mottram,F. C. II hite,Ef.Whittle, C. Melds, and J. Mimes.

THE POLITICAL • CRISIS.

MR. GLADSTONE'S RESIGNATION.

LORD ROSEBERY THE NEW PREMIER.

The "Court Circular" makes the official an- nouncement that after a Council held at Windsor on Saturday, the Right Hon. W. E. .Gladstone had an audience, of the Queen and tendered his rt-signa- tion, which was graciously accepted by Her Majesty, and that the Queen has offered the Earl of Rosebery the post-, of Prime Minister and his Lordship has accepted the offer. The new Cabinet met on Sunday at Lord Rosebery's house, and after the meeting the Premier sent a message to the Queen announcing the completion of the Ministry. The Earl of Rosebery had an audience of the Queen at Buckingham Palace on Monday, and kissed hands on his appointment as Prime Minister. It was afterwards officially announced that the Queen had been pleased to appoint the noble Earl First Lord of the Treasury and Lord Presideut of the Council, and the Earl of Kimberley Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs. Mr. John Morley has decided to remain at the Irish Office, and Mr. Fowler is to be the Secretary for India. Mr. T. Ellis succeeds Mr. Marjoribanks (now Lord Tweed- mouth) as Chief Liberal Whip. A number of hon- ours are announced in connection with the retire- ment of Mr. Gladstone. Mr. Stuart Rendel, M.P. for Montgomeryshire, and Sir Reginald Earle Welby (Permanent Secretary to the Treasury) are made peers, Sir Algernon West a Privy Councillor Mr. John Cowan of Beeslack (chairman of the Midloth- ian Liberal Association) a baronet, and Mr. Spencer Lyttleton and Mr. G. H. M urray (private secretaries to Mr. Gladstone) Commanders of the Bath. Mr. Labouchere states that he does not intend to move or support a resolution of want of confidence in the Government. The National Liberal Association has issued a manifesto to the Liberal Associations of the Coun- try, in which, after paying a high tribute to Mr. Gladstone's services, it says that the Ministry has abundantly proved that it is composed of strong and earliest men, and. can be trusted to conduct the affairs of the Empire with dignity and success. Such men, the document adds, will face with sympathy and courage the vast and pressing social problems by which we are confronted.

MR. GLADSTONE'S INDISPOSITION.

Mr. Gladstone is slightly indisposed. On Tuesday night he caught a cold, which produced hoarseness; and Dr. Bond considered it prudent that he should remain in bed on Wednesday. On Thursday morning the following bulletin was issued :—" Mr. Gladstone is better. Temperature 99 deg. No increase of bronchitis. Cough quieter."— "T. BOND, F.R,C.S.'

Mr. Gladstone passed a comfortable day. Dr. Bond visited his patient at half-past six on Thursday even- ing, and remained about a quarter of an hour. Dr. Bond said that Mr. Gladstone's cough was less troublesome, but the general condition was un- changed from the morning report.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW APPOINTMENTS.

It was officially stated on Thursday that Her Majesty has approved of the following further appointments :—Lord Tweedmouth to be Lord Privy Seal, with a seat in the Cabinet; Mr. Herbert Glad- stone to be First Commissioner of Works and a Privy Councillor; Mr. Munro Ferguson, M.P., to be a Junior Lord of the Treasury. Oa accepting the position of First Commissioner of Works, Mr. Her- bert Gladstone telegraphed the fact to his political friends in Leeds, and started for that city later in the day to make arrangements for his re-election.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
A GREAT RECORD.

If the speech of Thursday week is to be Mr. Gla- dstone's last Parliamentary oration it will be sepa- rated by a little over sixty-one years from his first. It was on February the 21st, 1823, that' Mr. Glad- stone first spoke in the House of Commons—on the Liverpool bribery petition. It is certainly a "re- cord " for any M.P.to have made his first speech at three-and-twenty and his last at eighty-four.

Lord Rosebery is says the "St. James Gazette," the new Prime Minister of England, the ruling Minister of the British Empire. What manner of man is he?

- . us past record shows a curious alternation be- ween publicity and reserve. Nothing could be, ap- parently, less reserved than the conduct of the man who for nearly three years dominated with his per- sonality the London County Council. Easy in his manners with everybody,keenly interested in every detail of the work he had. undertaken, clear-headed and large-minded, he was the very ideal of a muni- eipal statesman. The County Council adored him. Such a Chairman they are never likely to see again —genial, witty, strong, ever ready with the right phrase at the right moment. But in another sphere lArd Rosebery has been the very type of aristocra- tic reserve. More than any Foreign Secretary of modern times he has shrouded himself in a veil of silence, and has made the excellent rule that dip- lomatists should not talk about their business an excuse for not talking at all. On the County Coun- cil such a rule was impossible. If their Chairman had not told them what to do the untrained coun- cillors would have floundered into hopeless bogs. But except for this experience Lord Rosebery, like

Mr.. As,quith in the Lower House has been essentially a man of few speeches. Both men speak : pre-eminently well, and both keep up their standard by speaking seldom. For the coun- try as well as for the Liberal party it is encourag- ing fact that, in the period of universal palaver, the two most powerful men among the younger genera- tion of Liberals are the two men who talk the least. But to return to Lord Rosebery, the time that he has saved by not talking he has filled by doing. If the report is true, he has carried into the Foreign Office the same determined industry that he showed at Spring-gardens. The country only vaguely rea- lises the dangerous crisis it passed through last sum- mar. More than once during the long negotiations with France there was a possibility that we might be forced into war. On Lord Rosebery fell practi- cally the whole burden of that. time of stress. 'How much the strain told on the Foreign Secretary his al'ered physique shows. A few years ago, Lord Rosebery was as "fat "as Hamlet and almost cherub faced. Within the last six months he has become a comparatively Jean man, and constantly wears an expression that no one could call jovial.

LORD ROSEBERY. "THE MAN OF THE MOMENT."

===== 0002601_18940310_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
A YOUNG PREMIER.

Lord Rosebery is very youtig for an English Prime Minister. He is only forty-seven. But his public career already dates back nearly a quarter of a century. He succeeded to the title at the age of twenty-one, and ever since he has been actively and prominently engaged in political affairs. He seconded the reply to the address in 1871, and began his work as House of Lords reformer as far back as '74, when he moved for a Committee (of which became chairman) on Scotch and Irish representa- tive peerages. He entered office as Under-secretary for Home Affairs in 1881, and became First Commis- sioner of Works in 1884. In Mr. Gladstone's Government of 1886 he was Foreign Secretary. Lord Rosehery married 'Miss Hannah de Rothschild in 1878, and became a widower four years ago.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
PARNELLITE MANIFESTO.

' Mr. John Redmond, M.P., and his colleagues of the Parnellite party have issued a manifesto on the political. situation "to the Irish people." They hold that Mr. Gladstone's retirement can only have had its origin in a scheme to defeat the hopes of the Trial) people by the abandonment of Home Rule, or by such compromise as can only he regarded by Ireland as an insult an a mock- ery. They state that in Lord Rosebery and his Csbinet Ireland can have no confidence, and they warn their fellow countrymen to have none. It is a Cabinet, they maintain, that will concede jest as much to Ireland as she extorts by organization and independence; and they conclude by dem3 riding that these forces shall be set in motion, so that Ireland shall no longer be the scorn of one English party and the deluded dupe of the other.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHIP CANAL.

THE BATING OF. TEE CANAL.

At an adjourned meetineof the representatives of Assessment Committees of Unions in Lancashire and Cheshire bordering on the Ship Canal, held with re:fer- ence to the assessment of the waterway, the protest of the directors of the Ship Canal against the proposed rat- ing of the canal on an assessment of £1,500 a mile for a period of two years was read, and on the motion of Mr. Sutton (Warrington), seconded by the Chairman (Mr. Charles Reynolds, Runcorn), it was resolved " that the two years' term of rating ba abandoned,and that for the next rate the waterway, including the locks, be asse,sed at a rate of not less than £5OO per mile, and that so far as the recommendations as to combined action are con- cerned they apply. only as from the time of instructing a valuer."

===== 0002601_18940310_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
ARRIVAL OF THE BLACK PRINCE AT MANCHESTER.

The chief arrival at Manchester. on Thursday. was the Black Prince from New Orleans with cotton. The vessel is not so large as the previous cotton ships which have visited this port, and she only draws 14ft.61n. of water. The cargo is composed of 3,546 bales of cotton, 2,000 oil cakes, and 60 barrels of wine. She was built in 1883 by Messrs. Palmer and Company, of Jarrow, and left New Orkans on the 7th of February, being snfely berthed in the Salford Docks by eleven o'clock cn Thursday morn- ing. The gross tonnage is 1,480, and th 2 net tonnage 960. Captain Walsh reports a very stormy passage. After being at sea five days a storm arose and continued throughout the whole passage. The captain thinks the canal a great undertaking, and fully believes in its suc- cess. Tho voyage up the canal was accemplished prac- tically without any damage being done.

===== 0002601_18940310_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
A RUNCORN MAN CRUSHED TO DEATH AT MANCHESTER.

An accident unfortunately attended with loss of life occurred on Tuesday afternoon in the Pomona Dock of the Ship Canal, at Manchester. The Swan steamship from London, with a general cargo was entering the dock when it became necessary to run a rope from the shore to the starboard bow. For this purpose, John Sinnot, a. boatman of the Halloway, Runcorn, and aged 30 years, ontered a small boat, accompanied by an as- sistant. As the former was in the act of landing the rope, the ship swim.' round, and crushed the bolt against the pier wall. It is probable that Sinnot va, killed instantaneously, for he sank at once ; his asss- taht anticipating an accident, plunged into the water, and swam out of harm's way. He was then drawn from the water quite uninjured. The efforts to recover the body of the deceased have:been unsuccessful. The unfortunate young man who has thus met with his death in such a shocking manner, was the son of Mr. Sinnot, of New Ferry, who piloted the Sophie Wil mina up the Manchester Ship Canal.

THE SHIP CANAL COMPANY'S Acr. At a meeting of the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority on Tuesday, the Clerk reported that he had received a copy of the Ship Canal Company's Act, and the follow- ing clause might interest the Authority: "If any per- son casts, places, or deposits, or permits to be placed, cast, or deposited in any part of the canal docks or basins of the company forming part of the harbour and port of Manchester any solid matter or sludge, he shall be liable for every such offence not exceeding £5O ; and in addition thereto the Company may recover sum- marily from such person the cost of dredging or remov- ing such solid matter or sludge." Continuing, the clerk said the question arose " What is sludge ?" The Runcorn Improvement Commissioners were taking ac- tion with reference to the clause, and if it affected them it -would also affect that Authority. They had, in cer- tain agreements, power to turn matter into the Canal, and it was a question whether the At of Parliament would not override such agreements. They should have a clause to protect their own districts. Perhaps the canal company would agree to their request without any demur. He understood from the law clerk of the Runcorn Commissioners that they were prepared to in- sert a clause.—Mr.Aaron said that at Weston Point they had several sewers and also at Frodsham. If the Act passed in its present form the canal company could stop them.—The Rev. J. Hanby thought it would he well to join the committee of the Runcorn Commissioners and confer with them upon the different points.—The Clerk said the Runcoln Commissioners also objected to a clause with reference to the supply of water.—Mr. Williams expressed the opinion that they should also go against the clause in question,—Eventually, the subject was entrusted to a committee consisting of Messrs.Mclntyre, Heywood, Williaßas, Woodward,Baxter, Stokea,Norman, Jenkins, Brandreth, J. W. Baguley, and the Rev. J. Hanby.

ATHLETIO6.

RUGBY FOOTE/q.t..

Warrington's victory over Barrow on Saturday riag not a very easy one. Still, it is another "feather in .the wirepullers caps." and will have a tendency to render the concluding stages of the'Luicashire Cup Compatittoiz more interesting than ever. Of course the issue practi- cally lies between Swinton and Oldham, but a slip on .the pare of 'either of thesa teams will leave an opening' through which our home team might squeeze themselves and claim at length the object of their quest.

The game at Barrow was tame—as tame as the wild beasts in a menagorie, someone said. It kk as a forvimr.d game, except where scoring was concerned, and then lt was "backward." Capital combination was shown, by• both sets of forwards, but especially by Warrington,wha at times completely swept their opponents along the field There was no half-back play worthy of the name,for botb . sets watched each other so closely that nothing in the way of "passing out"- could be achieved. In the latter half of Vie game Barrow had most of their own way, although they failed to score. This was contrary to Warrington's. usual form, which is generally the best in the second, half of the game.

At full back Heesom gave a splendid exhibition. Massey displayed his "clean " heels to the delight of the "gate," but there was no three quartcr play even worth -; speaking of. Among the for wards Turner's Tittnic body was always to be seen to the fore, closely followed by Nevins. and Berry. Altogether there was not much to , choose between in the play cf thm tw& teams. What I cannot understand—and many others are in the same difficulty—is the undignified position which Barrow holds I in the Cup Competition, in face of the excellent form of • • the club! BOATING.

The Warrington Sailing-6-It;bi;ave arrived at a decision. Happy institution ! It is to be hoped that none of them will arrive on a sand bank after it ! They discussed the question of altering the course of the forthcoming race, - and I suppose for a time the dehlte waxed hot and furl- ous. "Then up spoke the midshipnian," and proposed a compromise. It was to the effect that the race be eon- tinued round a buoy near Walton Arches, and then, instead of stopping oppose Litton's Mill, to go on to the end of Monks, Hall and Co.'s wharf. And it wasagreed upon. This, I have no doubt, will be a great improve, ment upon previous races—but there is room for stilt more improvements.

There will be ten boats in the race including three " innovations," which take the place of three boat. which have sailed their last voyage. Primrose, Bessie. and Mi4chief, have all by a strange fatality, been bet. The owner of Primrose, Mr. W. Knowles, has, however. entered " Siesta,"and St.Cieorge has been entered by Mr. • G. Grounds, whilst the other vacancy has been filled by a boat owned by Mr. Jefferson.

COUNTY OF CHESTER. CONTAGIOUS DISEASES (ANIMALS) ,CTS, 1878 AND 1886. CLEANSING AND .DISINFECTION OF MARKETS, FAIRS, SALEVARDS, .PLACES OF EXHIBITION, OR OTHER PLACES -USED FOR ANIMALS. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that- the i..7xecutive Committee of the Local Authority of this County, at a Meeting held at the Grewe Arms Hotel, Crewe, on Thursday, the Ist day of March, 1894. ORDERED—That the eriPsting Regulations made on the 30:h day of June, 18a4., as to the Cleanciog and Disinfection of Markets, Fairs, Saleyards, Places of Exhibition, or other Places used for Animals, should be amended by providing that the Owners, Lesrees, or Occupiers of such Markets, 'Fairs, Saleyards, Places of Exhibition, Sze., should Cleanse and Disinfect the same. to the satisfaction of the auspector • for the District, within 48 hours from the conclusion of such Karket, Fair, Sale. or Exhitionof Animalsokc., such Cleansing and Disinfection being •carried out in the following manner, viz :- 1. By the sweeping out and effectually removicg all dung, sawdust, litter, and.other matter. 2. By the thorough cleansing thereof with water. 3. And if and when required by an Inspector, all parts of such places used As before-mentioned r4hall be well sprinkled over with a solution of catholic acid and water, in the following proportions' vi, half-a-pint of corbolic aekt well mixed with each gallon of water. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the theshire County Council. Cheater, Ist March, 1894.

WEEKLY CHAT.—The Pantheon is tilled to overfloWing with real genuine good SECOND-HAND FURNITURE, at Prices that simply bewilder and demoralise competition. The resources of The Pantheon are simply un- exhaustible. We reproduce a letter written on the 15th of January last, which aptly describes the advantages of The Pantheon:— [Copy.), Xfl.7ltrary 15th, 1894, Southport Dear Mrs. E.—l have delightful news to tell you. Jack has boughtthe furniture, and we feel gratified that we belie-e we have done well in our purchases. We have secured every thing we require from "The Pantheon,". and though we never expected to reach our ambition en the limited amount we agreed to expend, we are qpite satisfied. Truly some of th- heavy furniture is sr,cond-hand, but' renovating and re- upholstering has made them quite equal to new. We bought the Dining-room Furniture, which was made by Ogden and cost £l2O for £45. It consists of a massive suite, a most lovely sloe board with Dining Table and Overmantel to • match, it is in old Spanish mahogany, matured to a dark rich colour ; we can have our money returned if not satisfactory. I may say that I saw a large number of suites that would have suited at £20,t0 £3O, but we feel in love with the one £45. You would hardly credit the enormous variety in .diningroom suites they have at" The Pantheon." I believe there are • always over 160 on view. The Drawingroom, • Showroom is really charming, and I should like to have pur- chased several pieces of Chippendale Furniture, be, for economy, we bought a Sheraton Suite with a dear little settee, two. odd Lounge chairs, and a set of occa- sional chairs, all different designs, but harmonising in ,colour. The cabinet is just what I desired, and must have ,float three times as much AO we paid for it. Two Occasional , Tables, Overmantel, and Coal Vase completed the lot for £3B. I must tell 3ou about the bedr oms. We furnished only two for the present, one in American walnut and the other in lancewood. The walnut cost *.:40, we paid £22. The lance- wood, we paid 16 guirpus for, and I think it is worth double. I won't tell you about the bedsteads, bedding, carpets, cur- tains. ornaments, and all the sundries You must come when I the louse is fmished and see everything for yourself, which is promised within a week, We must thank Jack's cousin for recommending us to " The Pantheon," and I cannot do better than suggest that you should pay them a visit at the earliest op. ortunity. With love, and hoping to see you the moment we are ready to receive visitors.—Yours sincerely, FLORRIE, Thousands of bargains on view at the "The Pantheon," Read the following testimonial : Cannon-street, Manchrster, March 11, 1893.
